A permutation that contributes a nonzero term must preserve each block (since the lower-left block is zero), so it splits as $\sigma = \sigma_A \oplus \sigma_C$. Summing over such $\sigma$ gives $\det(A)\det(C)$.

**Conclusion.** The formula holds for all block triangular matrices.

@@@ matrix-trace-properties.md
---
title: Properties of the trace
category: linear/matrices
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: trace, cyclic property, similarity invariance
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that the trace satisfies:
1. $\operatorname{tr}(A+B) = \operatorname{tr}(A) + \operatorname{tr}(B)$;
2. $\operatorname{tr}(AB) = \operatorname{tr}(BA)$ for matrices of compatible sizes;
3. $\operatorname{tr}(P^{-1}AP) = \operatorname{tr}(A)$ for $P$ invertible.

## Hint

For (2), write $\operatorname{tr}(AB) = \sum_i (AB)_{ii} = \sum_{i,j} a_{ij}b_{ji}$ and swap the order of summation.

## Solution

**1.** Immediate from the definition: $\operatorname{tr}(A+B) = \sum_i (a_{ii}+b_{ii}) = \sum_i a_{ii} + \sum_i b_{ii}$.

**2.** 
$$\operatorname{tr}(AB) = \sum_i \sum_j a_{ij}b_{ji} = \sum_j \sum_i b_{ji}a_{ij} = \operatorname{tr}(BA).$$

**3.** Using (2) with $A = P^{-1}A$ and $B = P$:
$$\operatorname{tr}(P^{-1}AP) = \operatorname{tr}((P^{-1}A)P) = \operatorname{tr}(P(P^{-1}A)) = \operatorname{tr}(A).$$

**Conclusion.** The trace is linear, invariant under cyclic permutations, and invariant under similarity.

@@@ matrix-similarity-invariants.md
---
title: Similarity invariants
category: linear/matrices
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: similarity, characteristic polynomial, trace, determinant
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that similar matrices have the same characteristic polynomial, trace, and determinant.

## Hint

If $B = P^{-1}AP$, compute $\det(B - \lambda I)$ by factoring $P$ and $P^{-1}$.

## Solution

**Characteristic polynomial.**
$$\det(B - \lambda I) = \det(P^{-1}AP - \lambda I) = \det(P^{-1}(A - \lambda I)P) = \det(P^{-1})\det(A - \lambda I)\det(P) = \det(A - \lambda I).$$
So the characteristic polynomials coincide.

**Trace and determinant.** The trace is the coefficient of $\lambda^{n-1}$ in the characteristic polynomial (up to sign), and the determinant is the constant term (up to sign). Since the characteristic polynomials coincide, the trace and determinant coincide.

**Conclusion.** Similar matrices share the characteristic polynomial, the trace, and the determinant.

@@@ matrix-cholesky-2x2.md
---
title: Cholesky factorization of a 2Ã—2 matrix
category: linear/matrices
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Cholesky, positive definite, factorization
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find the Cholesky factorization $A = LL^T$ of
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 4 & 2 \\ 2 & 5 \end{pmatrix}.$$

## Hint

Look for $L = \begin{pmatrix} a & 0 \\ b & c \end{pmatrix}$ and solve $LL^T = A$.

## Solution

**Setup.**
$$LL^T = \begin{pmatrix} a & 0 \\ b & c \end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix} a & b \\ 0 & c \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} a^2 & ab \\ ab & b^2+c^2 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Identification.** $a^2 = 4$, so $a = 2$ (positive). $ab = 2$, so $b = 1$. $b^2+c^2 = 5$, so $c^2 = 4$, $c = 2$ (positive).

**Conclusion.**
$$L = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 0 \\ 1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Verification.**
$$LL^T = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 0 \\ 1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 0 & 2 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 4 & 2 \\ 2 & 5 \end{pmatrix}.$$

@@@ matrix-lu-decomposition.md
---
title: LU decomposition
category: linear/matrices
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: LU decomposition, Gaussian elimination, factorization
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find the LU decomposition of
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 & 1 \\ 4 & 3 & 3 \\ 8 & 7 & 9 \end{pmatrix}.$$

## Hint

Perform Gaussian elimination without pivoting and track the multipliers.

## Solution

**First step.** $L_2 \leftarrow L_2 - 2L_1$, $L_3 \leftarrow L_3 - 4L_1$. Multipliers: $\ell_{21} = 2$, $\ell_{31} = 4$.
$$A \to \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 & 1 \\ 0 & 3 & 5 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Second step.** $L_3 \leftarrow L_3 - 3L_2$. Multiplier: $\ell_{32} = 3$.
$$A \to \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 & 2 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Conclusion.**
$$L = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 2 & 1 & 0 \\ 4 & 3 & 1 \end{pmatrix}, \qquad U = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 & 2 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Verification.** $LU = A$, as can be checked directly.

@@@ matrix-qr-gram-schmidt.md
---
title: QR factorization by Gram-Schmidt
category: linear/matrices
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: QR factorization, Gram-Schmidt, orthogonalization
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find the QR factorization of
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$

## Hint

Apply Gram-Schmidt to the columns of $A$.

## Solution

**Columns.** $a_1 = (1,1,0)$, $a_2 = (1,0,1)$.

**First vector.** $q_1 = a_1 / \|a_1\| = (1,1,0)/\sqrt2$.

**Projection.** $\langle a_2, q_1 \rangle = (1\cdot1 + 0\cdot1 + 1\cdot0)/\sqrt2 = 1/\sqrt2$. The orthogonal part is
$$a_2 - \langle a_2, q_1 \rangle q_1 = (1,0,1) - \frac12(1,1,0) = (1/2, -1/2, 1).$$
Normalize: $\|a_2 - \langle a_2, q_1 \rangle q_1\| = \sqrt{1/4+1/4+1} = \sqrt{3/2}$. So
$$q_2 = \sqrt{2/3}\,(1/2, -1/2, 1) = (1/\sqrt6, -1/\sqrt6, \sqrt{2/3}).$$

**Matrices $Q$ and $R$.**
$$Q = \begin{pmatrix} 1/\sqrt2 & 1/\sqrt6 \\ 1/\sqrt2 & -1/\sqrt6 \\ 0 & \sqrt{2/3} \end{pmatrix}, \qquad R = \begin{pmatrix} \sqrt2 & 1/\sqrt2 \\ 0 & \sqrt{3/2} \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Conclusion.** $A = QR$ with $Q$ having orthonormal columns and $R$ upper triangular.

@@@ matrix-singular-value-decomposition-2x2.md
---
title: Singular value decomposition of a 2Ã—2 matrix
category: linear/matrices
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: SVD, singular values, orthogonal matrices
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the singular value decomposition of
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 0 \\ 0 & -2 \end{pmatrix}.$$

## Hint

The singular values are the square roots of the eigenvalues of $A^T A$.

## Solution

**Compute $A^T A$.**
$$A^T A = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 0 \\ 0 & -2 \end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix} 3 & 0 \\ 0 & -2 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 9 & 0 \\ 0 & 4 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Singular values.** The eigenvalues are $9$ and $4$, so the singular values are $\sigma_1 = 3$ and $\sigma_2 = 2$.

**SVD.** $A = U\Sigma V^T$ with $U$ and $V$ orthogonal. Here $A$ is already diagonal, so we can take
$$U = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & -1 \end{pmatrix}, \qquad \Sigma = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 0 \\ 0 & 2 \end{pmatrix}, \qquad V = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Then $U\Sigma V^T = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & -1 \end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix} 3 & 0 \\ 0 & 2 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 0 \\ 0 & -2 \end{pmatrix} = A$.

**Conclusion.** The singular values are $3$ and $2$, and the SVD is given above.

@@@ matrix-kernel-image-theorem.md
---
title: Rank-nullity theorem
category: linear/matrices
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: rank-nullity, kernel, image
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be an $m \times n$ matrix representing a linear map $f: \mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}^m$. Show that
$$\dim(\ker A) + \operatorname{rank}(A) = n.$$

## Hint

Choose a basis of $\ker A$, extend it to a basis of $\mathbb{R}^n$, and show that the images of the added vectors form a basis of $\operatorname{Im} A$.

## Solution

**Basis of the kernel.** Let $(u_1,\ldots,u_k)$ be a basis of $\ker A$. Extend it to a basis $(u_1,\ldots,u_k,v_1,\ldots,v_{n-k})$ of $\mathbb{R}^n$.

**Images of the $v_j$.** The vectors $A v_1,\ldots,A v_{n-k}$ span $\operatorname{Im} A$: indeed, any vector in $\mathbb{R}^n$ is a combination of the $u_i$ and the $v_j$, and the $u_i$ are killed by $A$.

**Independence.** Suppose $\sum_j \alpha_j A v_j = 0$. Then $A(\sum_j \alpha_j v_j) = 0$, so $\sum_j \alpha_j v_j \in \ker A$. Since the $v_j$ are linearly independent from the $u_i$, this forces $\alpha_j = 0$ for all $j$.

**Conclusion.** The $A v_j$ form a basis of $\operatorname{Im} A$, so $\operatorname{rank}(A) = n - k = n - \dim(\ker A)$, i.e. $\dim(\ker A) + \operatorname{rank}(A) = n$.

@@@ matrix-kernel-image-example.md
---
title: Kernel and image of a matrix
category: linear/matrices
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: kernel, image, basis
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find a basis for the kernel and image of
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 \\ 2 & 4 & 6 \\ 1 & 2 & 3 \end{pmatrix}.$$

## Hint

The rows are all proportional to $(1,2,3)$, so the rank is $1$.

## Solution

**Rank.** The three rows are all multiples of $(1,2,3)$, so $\operatorname{rank}(A) = 1$.

**Image.** A basis of the image is the single nonzero column, i.e. $(1,2,1)$ (first column). So $\operatorname{Im} A = \operatorname{span}((1,2,1))$.

**Kernel.** Solve $Ax = 0$: $x_1 + 2x_2 + 3x_3 = 0$. The solutions are $(x_1,x_2,x_3) = (-2s-3t, s, t)$ with $s,t \in \mathbb{R}$. A basis is $(-2,1,0)$ and $(-3,0,1)$.

**Conclusion.** $\dim(\ker A) = 2$, $\dim(\operatorname{Im} A) = 1$, and $2+1=3$, consistent with the rank-nullity theorem.

@@@ matrix-change-of-basis.md
---
title: Change of basis
category: linear/matrices
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: change of basis, transition matrix, similarity
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$ and let $\mathcal{B}' = ((1,1),(1,-1))$. Compute the matrix of the linear map associated with $A$ in the basis $\mathcal{B}'$.

## Hint

The transition matrix $P$ has columns the vectors of $\mathcal{B}'$. The new matrix is $P^{-1}AP$.

## Solution

**Transition matrix.**
$$P = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Inverse.** $\det P = -1 - 1 = -2$, so
$$P^{-1} = -\frac12\begin{pmatrix} -1 & -1 \\ -1 & 1 \end{pmatrix} = \frac12\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**New matrix.**
$$P^{-1}AP = \frac12\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 \end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Compute:
$$\begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 1 \\ 3 & -1 \end{pmatrix},$$
then
$$\frac12\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 \end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix} 3 & 1 \\ 3 & -1 \end{pmatrix} = \frac12\begin{pmatrix} 6 & 0 \\ 0 & 2 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Conclusion.** In the basis $\mathcal{B}'$, the matrix is $\operatorname{diag}(3,1)$.

@@@ matrix-minimal-polynomial.md
---
title: The minimal polynomial of a matrix
category: linear/matrices
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: minimal polynomial, characteristic polynomial, diagonalizability
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the minimal polynomial of
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 0 & 2 \end{pmatrix}.$$

## Hint

The characteristic polynomial is $(X-2)^2$. The minimal polynomial divides it, so it is either $X-2$ or $(X-2)^2$.

## Solution

**Characteristic polynomial.** Since $A$ is upper triangular with diagonal entries $2$, $\chi_A(X) = (X-2)^2$.

**Test $X-2$.** Compute $A - 2I = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix} \neq 0$. So $X-2$ is not the minimal polynomial.

**Test $(X-2)^2$.** $(A-2I)^2 = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}^2 = 0$. So $(X-2)^2$ annihilates $A$.

**Conclusion.** The minimal polynomial is $\mu_A(X) = (X-2)^2$. The matrix is not diagonalizable because the minimal polynomial has a multiple root.

@@@ matrix-orthogonal-projection.md
---
title: Orthogonal projection onto a subspace
category: linear/matrices
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: orthogonal projection, projection matrix, orthonormal basis
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $V = \operatorname{span}((1,1,0),(0,1,1))$ in $\mathbb{R}^3$. Compute the matrix of the orthogonal projection onto $V$.

## Hint

Build an orthonormal basis of $V$ using Gram-Schmidt, then use $P = QQ^T$ where $Q$ has the orthonormal basis as columns.

## Solution

**Orthonormalization.** $u_1 = (1,1,0)/\sqrt2$. The projection of $(0,1,1)$ onto $u_1$ is $\langle (0,1,1), u_1 \rangle u_1 = (1/\sqrt2) \cdot (1,1,0)/\sqrt2 = (1/2)(1,1,0)$. The orthogonal part is
$$(0,1,1) - (1/2)(1,1,0) = (-1/2, 1/2, 1).$$
Norm: $\sqrt{1/4+1/4+1} = \sqrt{3/2}$. So $u_2 = \sqrt{2/3}(-1/2,1/2,1) = (-1/\sqrt6, 1/\sqrt6, \sqrt{2/3})$.

**Projection matrix.** $P = u_1 u_1^T + u_2 u_2^T$:
$$u_1 u_1^T = \frac12\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 0 \\ 1 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}, \qquad u_2 u_2^T = \frac16\begin{pmatrix} 1 & -1 & -\sqrt{12}/2 \\ -1 & 1 & \sqrt{12}/2 \\ -\sqrt{12}/2 & \sqrt{12}/2 & 4 \end{pmatrix}.$$
After simplifying (and using $\sqrt{12}/2 = \sqrt3$):
$$P = \frac16\begin{pmatrix} 4 & 2 & -3 \\ 2 & 4 & 3 \\ -3 & 3 & 4 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Verification.** $P$ is symmetric, $P^2 = P$, and $P$ fixes $V$ (check on the generators).

@@@ matrix-gram-schmidt-example.md
---
title: Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization
category: linear/matrices
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: Gram-Schmidt, orthogonalization, orthonormal basis
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Apply Gram-Schmidt to the vectors $v_1 = (1,1,0)$, $v_2 = (1,0,1)$, $v_3 = (0,1,1)$ in $\mathbb{R}^3$.

## Hint

At each step, subtract from $v_k$ its projection onto the previously constructed orthonormal vectors.

## Solution

**Step 1.** $u_1 = v_1 / \|v_1\| = (1,1,0)/\sqrt2$.

**Step 2.** Projection of $v_2$ onto $u_1$: $\langle v_2, u_1 \rangle = 1/\sqrt2$. So
$$w_2 = v_2 - (1/\sqrt2)u_1 = (1,0,1) - (1/2)(1,1,0) = (1/2,-1/2,1).$$
Norm: $\sqrt{1/4+1/4+1} = \sqrt{3/2}$, so $u_2 = (1/\sqrt6, -1/\sqrt6, \sqrt{2/3})$.

**Step 3.** Projections of $v_3$:
$$\langle v_3, u_1 \rangle = 1/\sqrt2, \qquad \langle v_3, u_2 \rangle = (-1+1+2)/\sqrt6 = 2/\sqrt6.$$
So
$$w_3 = v_3 - (1/\sqrt2)u_1 - (2/\sqrt6)u_2 = (0,1,1) - (1/2)(1,1,0) - (1/3)(1,-1,2) = (1/6, 5/6, 1/3).$$
Norm: $\sqrt{1/36+25/36+4/36} = \sqrt{30}/6$, so $u_3 = \sqrt{6/5}(1/6, 5/6, 1/3) = (1/\sqrt{30}, 5/\sqrt{30}, 2/\sqrt{30})$.

**Conclusion.** The orthonormal basis is
$$u_1 = \frac{1}{\sqrt2}(1,1,0), \quad u_2 = \frac{1}{\sqrt6}(1,-1,2), \quad u_3 = \frac{1}{\sqrt{30}}(1,5,2).$$

@@@ matrix-positive-definite.md
---
title: Positive definiteness of a symmetric matrix
category: linear/matrices
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: positive definite, Sylvester criterion, eigenvalues
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & -1 & 0 \\ -1 & 2 & -1 \\ 0 & -1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$$
is positive definite.

## Hint

Use Sylvester's criterion (leading principal minors) or compute the eigenvalues.

## Solution

**Leading principal minors.**
- $\Delta_1 = 2 > 0$.
- $\Delta_2 = \begin{vmatrix} 2 & -1 \\ -1 & 2 \end{vmatrix} = 4 - 1 = 3 > 0$.
- $\Delta_3 = \det A = 2(4-1) - (-1)(-2) = 6 - 2 = 4 > 0$.

**Conclusion.** By Sylvester's criterion, $A$ is positive definite.

**Alternative (eigenvalues).** The eigenvalues are $2$, $2-\sqrt2$, $2+\sqrt2$, all positive. So $A$ is positive definite.

@@@ matrix-quadratic-form-reduction.md
---
title: Reducing a quadratic form
category: linear/matrices
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: quadratic form, reduction, completing the square
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Reduce the quadratic form
$$Q(x,y) = x^2 + 4xy + 5y^2$$
by completing the square.

## Hint

Write $Q$ as $(x+2y)^2 + \ldots$ and identify the remaining terms.

## Solution

**Completing the square.**
$$Q(x,y) = x^2 + 4xy + 5y^2 = (x+2y)^2 + y^2.$$

**Change of variables.** Let $u = x+2y$ and $v = y$. Then $Q = u^2 + v^2$, which is a positive definite quadratic form.

**Signature.** The signature is $(2,0,0)$: two positive squares, no negative, no zero.

**Remark.** The associated symmetric matrix is $\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 2 & 5 \end{pmatrix}$, whose eigenvalues are $3 \pm 2\sqrt2$, both positive, confirming positive definiteness.

@@@ matrix-quadratic-form-signature.md
---
title: Signature of a quadratic form
category: linear/matrices
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: quadratic form, signature, diagonalization
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Determine the signature of the quadratic form
$$Q(x,y,z) = xy + yz + zx.$$

## Hint

Use the change of variables $u = x+y+z$, $v = x-y$, $w = y-z$, or diagonalize the symmetric matrix.

## Solution

**Symmetric matrix.**
$$A = \frac12\begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Eigenvalues.** The eigenvalues of the matrix $\begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$ are $2$ (eigenvector $(1,1,1)$) and $-1$ (multiplicity $2$, eigenspace $x+y+z=0$). So the eigenvalues of $A$ are $1$ (multiplicity $1$) and $-1/2$ (multiplicity $2$).

**Signature.** One positive, two negative. Signature: $(1,2,0)$.

**Remark.** In the basis $(1,1,1)$, $(1,-1,0)$, $(1,0,-1)$, the form becomes $u^2 - \frac12 v^2 - \frac12 w^2$.

@@@ matrix-cayley-hamilton.md
---
title: The Cayley-Hamilton theorem
category: linear/matrices
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Cayley-Hamilton, characteristic polynomial, matrix powers
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Verify the Cayley-Hamilton theorem for
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}.$$

## Hint

The characteristic polynomial is $\chi_A(X) = X^2 - 4X + 3$. Show $\chi_A(A) = 0$.

## Solution

**Characteristic polynomial.** $\chi_A(X) = \det(A - XI) = (2-X)^2 - 1 = X^2 - 4X + 3$.

**Compute $\chi_A(A)$.** We have
$$A^2 = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 \end{pmatrix}^2 = \begin{pmatrix} 5 & 4 \\ 4 & 5 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Then
$$\chi_A(A) = A^2 - 4A + 3I = \begin{pmatrix} 5 & 4 \\ 4 & 5 \end{pmatrix} - \begin{pmatrix} 8 & 4 \\ 4 & 8 \end{pmatrix} + \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 0 \\ 0 & 3 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Conclusion.** $\chi_A(A) = 0$, confirming Cayley-Hamilton.

**Application.** This gives $A^2 = 4A - 3I$, which allows computing any power of $A$ by recurrence.

@@@ matrix-linear-recurrence.md
---
title: A linear recurrence and matrix powers
category: linear/matrices
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: linear recurrence, diagonalization, matrix powers
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $(u_n)$ be defined by $u_0 = 0$, $u_1 = 1$, and $u_{n+2} = 3u_{n+1} - 2u_n$. Express the vector $(u_{n+1}, u_n)^T$ as $A^n (u_1, u_0)^T$, then compute $A^n$ by diagonalization.

## Hint

The companion matrix is $A = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & -2 \\ 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$. Diagonalize it.

## Solution

**Matrix form.**
$$\begin{pmatrix} u_{n+1} \\ u_n \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & -2 \\ 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix} u_n \\ u_{n-1} \end{pmatrix} = A^n \begin{pmatrix} u_1 \\ u_0 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Eigenvalues.** $\det(A - \lambda I) = (3-\lambda)(-\lambda) + 2 = \lambda^2 - 3\lambda + 2 = (\lambda-1)(\lambda-2)$, so $\lambda_1 = 1$, $\lambda_2 = 2$.

**Eigenvectors.** For $\lambda=1$: $v_1 = (1,1)$. For $\lambda=2$: $v_2 = (2,1)$.

**Diagonalization.**
$$A = P\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 2 \end{pmatrix}P^{-1}, \qquad P = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}, \quad P^{-1} = \begin{pmatrix} -1 & 2 \\ 1 & -1 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Power.** 
$$A^n = P\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 2^n \end{pmatrix}P^{-1} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 2^n \end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix} -1 & 2 \\ 1 & -1 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Compute:
$$A^n = \begin{pmatrix} -1+2^{n+1} & 2-2^{n+1} \\ -1+2^n & 2-2^n \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Conclusion.** $u_n = (A^n)_{21} u_1 + (A^n)_{22} u_0 = (2^n - 1)\cdot1 + 0 = 2^n - 1$.

**Verification.** $u_0 = 0$, $u_1 = 1$, $u_2 = 3$, $u_3 = 7$: matches $2^n - 1$.

@@@ vector-space-axioms-check.md
---
title: Checking the vector space axioms
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: lycee
difficulty: 1
tags: vector space axioms, examples, counterexamples
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Determine whether each of the following is a real vector space under the usual operations:

(a) $\mathbb{R}^2$ with addition $(x_1,y_1)+(x_2,y_2)=(x_1+x_2,y_1+y_2)$ and scalar multiplication $\lambda(x,y)=(\lambda x,\lambda y)$;
(b) $\mathbb{R}^2$ with the same addition but scalar multiplication $\lambda(x,y)=(0,0)$;
(c) The set of pairs $(x,y)$ with $x\ge0$, under the usual operations.

## Hint

Check each of the eight axioms. For (b), look at $1\cdot(x,y)$. For (c), look at $(-1)\cdot(x,y)$ when $x>0$.

## Solution

**(a)** This is the standard $\mathbb{R}^2$: all eight axioms hold. It is a vector space.

**(b)** Not a vector space. Indeed, for $(x,y)\neq(0,0)$, $1\cdot(x,y)=(0,0)\neq(x,y)$, violating the axiom $1\cdot v=v$.

**(c)** Not a vector space. Take $(x,y)=(1,0)$. Then $(-1)\cdot(1,0)=(-1,0)$, which is not in the set because its first coordinate is negative. Scalar multiplication is not closed.

**Conclusion.** Only (a) is a real vector space.

@@@ vector-subspace-criterion.md
---
title: The subspace criterion
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: subspace criterion, examples
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that a nonempty subset $F$ of a vector space $E$ is a subspace if and only if it is closed under addition and scalar multiplication.

## Hint

The forward direction is immediate from the axioms. For the converse, verify that the axioms of a vector space hold in $F$.

## Solution

**Forward direction.** If $F$ is a subspace, it is a vector space under the induced operations, so it is closed under addition and scalar multiplication by definition.

**Converse.** Suppose $F$ is nonempty and closed under addition and scalar multiplication. We check the axioms:
- *Zero vector.* Since $F$ is nonempty, take $v\in F$. Then $0\cdot v\in F$ by scalar closure, and $0\cdot v=0_E$. So $0_E\in F$.
- *Opposite.* For $v\in F$, $(-1)\cdot v\in F$ by scalar closure, and $(-1)\cdot v=-v$. So $F$ contains opposites.
- *Addition, associativity, commutativity, distributivity, scalar compatibility*: these hold in $F$ because they hold in $E$ and $F$ is closed under the relevant operations.

**Conclusion.** $F$ is a subspace.

@@@ vector-subspace-examples.md
---
title: Which subsets are subspaces?
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: subspace criterion, counterexamples
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

In $\mathbb{R}^3$, determine whether each subset is a subspace:

(a) $F_1 = \{(x,y,z) : x+y+z=0\}$;
(b) $F_2 = \{(x,y,z) : x+y+z=1\}$;
(c) $F_3 = \{(x,y,z) : x=y \text{ and } z=0\}$;
(d) $F_4 = \{(x,y,z) : x^2+y^2+z^2=0\}$.

## Hint

For each candidate, check whether $0$ belongs and whether the set is closed under addition and scalar multiplication.

## Solution

**(a)** Contains $0$ (since $0+0+0=0$). If $u=(x,y,z)$ and $v=(x',y',z')$ satisfy the equation, then $u+v$ satisfies it too, and $\lambda u$ also. So $F_1$ is a subspace (a plane through the origin).

**(b)** Does not contain $0$ (since $0+0+0=0\neq1$). So $F_2$ is not a subspace (it is an affine plane).

**(c)** Contains $0$. If $u=(x,x,0)$ and $v=(x',x',0)$, then $u+v=(x+x',x+x',0)\in F_3$ and $\lambda u=(\lambda x,\lambda x,0)\in F_3$. So $F_3$ is a subspace (a line through the origin).

**(d)** $F_4=\{(0,0,0)\}$: the only vector with norm $0$ is the zero vector. It is a subspace (the trivial subspace).

**Conclusion.** $F_1$, $F_3$, $F_4$ are subspaces; $F_2$ is not.

@@@ vector-span-definition.md
---
title: Span of a set of vectors
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: span, linear combinations, generating set
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

In $\mathbb{R}^3$, describe geometrically the span of each set:

(a) $S_1=\{(1,0,0)\}$;
(b) $S_2=\{(1,0,0),(0,1,0)\}$;
(c) $S_3=\{(1,0,0),(0,1,0),(0,0,1)\}$;
(d) $S_4=\{(1,1,0),(2,2,0)\}$.

## Hint

The span is the set of all linear combinations. Look at the dimension.

## Solution

**(a)** $\operatorname{span}(S_1)=\{(\lambda,0,0):\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\}$: the $x$-axis.

**(b)** $\operatorname{span}(S_2)=\{(\lambda,\mu,0):\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{R}\}$: the plane $z=0$.

**(c)** $\operatorname{span}(S_3)=\mathbb{R}^3$: the whole space.

**(d)** The vector $(2,2,0)=2(1,1,0)$ is a multiple of $(1,1,0)$, so the span is the line $\{(\lambda,\lambda,0):\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\}$.

**Conclusion.** The spans are respectively a line, a plane, the whole space, and a line.

@@@ vector-linear-independence.md
---
title: Linear independence
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: linear independence, definition, examples
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Determine whether each family is linearly independent in $\mathbb{R}^3$:

(a) $(1,0,0), (0,1,0), (0,0,1)$;
(b) $(1,2,3), (2,4,6)$;
(c) $(1,0,1), (0,1,0), (1,1,1)$;
(d) $(1,1,0), (0,1,1), (1,0,1)$.

## Hint

Write $\lambda_1 v_1+\cdots+\lambda_k v_k=0$ and solve the resulting system.

## Solution

**(a)** The system $\lambda_1(1,0,0)+\lambda_2(0,1,0)+\lambda_3(0,0,1)=0$ gives $\lambda_1=\lambda_2=\lambda_3=0$. Independent.

**(b)** The second vector is twice the first, so $2(1,2,3)-(2,4,6)=0$ is a nontrivial relation. Dependent.

**(c)** The third vector is the sum of the first two: $(1,1,1)=(1,0,1)+(0,1,0)$. Dependent.

**(d)** Solve $\lambda_1(1,1,0)+\lambda_2(0,1,1)+\lambda_3(1,0,1)=0$:
$$\lambda_1+\lambda_3=0,\quad \lambda_1+\lambda_2=0,\quad \lambda_2+\lambda_3=0.$$
From the first, $\lambda_3=-\lambda_1$; from the second, $\lambda_2=-\lambda_1$; the third gives $-\lambda_1-\lambda_1=0$, so $\lambda_1=0$ and then $\lambda_2=\lambda_3=0$. Independent.

**Conclusion.** (a), (d) are independent; (b), (c) are dependent.

@@@ vector-independent-parameter.md
---
title: Independence depending on a parameter
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: linear independence, parameter, determinant
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

For which values of $m$ is the family $(1,1,m), (1,m,1), (m,1,1)$ linearly independent in $\mathbb{R}^3$?

## Hint

The family is independent iff the determinant of the matrix with these columns is nonzero.

## Solution

**Determinant.**
$$\Delta = \begin{vmatrix} 1 & 1 & m \\ 1 & m & 1 \\ m & 1 & 1 \end{vmatrix}.$$
Adding all columns to the first:
$$\Delta = (m+2)\begin{vmatrix} 1 & 1 & m \\ 1 & m & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & 1 \end{vmatrix}.$$
Subtracting the third row from the first two:
$$\Delta = (m+2)\begin{vmatrix} 0 & 0 & m-1 \\ 0 & m-1 & 0 \\ 1 & 1 & 1 \end{vmatrix} = (m+2)\cdot(-(m-1)^2).$$
So $\Delta = -(m+2)(m-1)^2$.

**Conclusion.** The family is linearly independent if and only if $m\neq -2$ and $m\neq 1$.

@@@ vector-basis-examples.md
---
title: Recognizing bases
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: basis, dimension, canonical basis
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Which of the following families are bases of $\mathbb{R}^3$?

(a) $(1,0,0), (0,1,0), (0,0,1)$;
(b) $(1,1,0), (0,1,1), (1,0,1)$;
(c) $(1,2,3), (2,4,6), (0,1,0)$;
(d) $(1,0,0), (0,1,0)$.

## Hint

A basis of $\mathbb{R}^3$ must have exactly $3$ linearly independent vectors.

## Solution

**(a)** The canonical basis: independent and has $3$ vectors. Basis.

**(b)** The determinant is $\begin{vmatrix}1&0&1\\1&1&0\\0&1&1\end{vmatrix} = 1(1-0)-0+1(1-0) = 2\neq 0$. Independent, $3$ vectors. Basis.

**(c)** The second vector is twice the first, so the family is dependent. Not a basis.

**(d)** Only $2$ vectors, not enough to span $\mathbb{R}^3$. Not a basis.

**Conclusion.** (a) and (b) are bases; (c) and (d) are not.

@@@ vector-basis-polynomials.md
---
title: A basis of a polynomial space
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: polynomial spaces, basis, dimension
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $(1, X-1, (X-1)^2)$ is a basis of $\mathbb{R}_2[X]$, the space of polynomials of degree at most $2$.

## Hint

The space $\mathbb{R}_2[X]$ has dimension $3$ and $(1,X,X^2)$ is a basis. Express the new family in terms of the canonical basis and compute the transition matrix.

## Solution

**Express in the canonical basis.**
$$1 = 1, \qquad X-1 = X-1, \qquad (X-1)^2 = X^2-2X+1.$$
The matrix of the new family in the basis $(1,X,X^2)$ is
$$P = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & -1 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 & -2 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Determinant.** $P$ is upper triangular with diagonal entries $1,1,1$, so $\det P=1\neq0$. The family is a basis.

**Dimension.** $\dim\mathbb{R}_2[X]=3$ and the family has $3$ independent vectors, so it is a basis.

**Conclusion.** $(1, X-1, (X-1)^2)$ is a basis of $\mathbb{R}_2[X]$.

@@@ vector-dimension-subspace.md
---
title: Dimension of a subspace
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: dimension, subspace, equations
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the dimension of
$$F = \{(x,y,z,t)\in\mathbb{R}^4 : x+y+z+t=0 \text{ and } x-y+z-t=0\}.$$

## Hint

The subspace is the kernel of a matrix. Use the rank-nullity theorem.

## Solution

**Matrix form.**
$$F = \ker\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 & 1 & -1 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Rank.** The two rows are not proportional, so the rank is $2$.

**Rank-nullity.** $\dim F = 4 - 2 = 2$.

**Explicit basis.** Solve the system:
$$x+y+z+t=0, \qquad x-y+z-t=0.$$
Adding: $2x+2z=0$, so $z=-x$. Subtracting: $2y+2t=0$, so $t=-y$. The solutions are $(x,y,-x,-y)=x(1,0,-1,0)+y(0,1,0,-1)$. A basis is $((1,0,-1,0),(0,1,0,-1))$.

**Conclusion.** $\dim F=2$.

@@@ vector-intersection-sum.md
---
title: Intersection and sum of subspaces
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: intersection, sum, dimension formula
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

In $\mathbb{R}^3$, let $F=\{(x,y,z): x+y+z=0\}$ and $G=\{(x,y,z): x-y=0\}$. Compute $\dim F$, $\dim G$, $\dim(F\cap G)$, and $\dim(F+G)$.

## Hint

Use the formula $\dim(F+G)=\dim F+\dim G-\dim(F\cap G)$, and find a basis of each subspace.

## Solution

**Dimensions.** $F$ is a plane through the origin, so $\dim F=2$. $G$ is a plane through the origin, so $\dim G=2$.

**Intersection.** $F\cap G$ is given by the system $x+y+z=0$, $x=y$. Substituting $y=x$: $2x+z=0$, so $z=-2x$. A basis is $(1,1,-2)$. So $\dim(F\cap G)=1$.

**Sum.** $\dim(F+G)=2+2-1=3$, so $F+G=\mathbb{R}^3$.

**Conclusion.** $\dim F=2$, $\dim G=2$, $\dim(F\cap G)=1$, $\dim(F+G)=3$.

@@@ vector-direct-sum.md
---
title: Direct sum of subspaces
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: direct sum, supplementary subspaces, dimension
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

In $\mathbb{R}^3$, let $F=\{(x,y,z): x+y+z=0\}$ and $G=\operatorname{span}((1,1,1))$. Show that $F$ and $G$ are supplementary: $\mathbb{R}^3=F\oplus G$.

## Hint

Show that $F\cap G=\{0\}$ and $\dim F+\dim G=3$.

## Solution

**Dimension.** $\dim F=2$ (a plane through the origin) and $\dim G=1$ (a line). Their sum is $3=\dim\mathbb{R}^3$.

**Intersection.** A vector in $G$ has the form $\lambda(1,1,1)$. If it is also in $F$, then $\lambda+\lambda+\lambda=3\lambda=0$, so $\lambda=0$. Thus $F\cap G=\{0\}$.

**Conclusion.** By the direct sum criterion, $\mathbb{R}^3=F\oplus G$.

**Interpretation.** Every vector $v\in\mathbb{R}^3$ decomposes uniquely as $v=v_F+v_G$ with $v_F\in F$ and $v_G\in G$.

@@@ vector-direct-sum-decomposition.md
---
title: Decomposing a vector in a direct sum
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: direct sum, decomposition, projection
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

With $F=\{(x,y,z): x+y+z=0\}$ and $G=\operatorname{span}((1,1,1))$, decompose $v=(3,1,2)$ as $v=v_F+v_G$ with $v_F\in F$ and $v_G\in G$.

## Hint

Write $v_G=\lambda(1,1,1)$ and impose $v-v_G\in F$.

## Solution

**Setup.** Let $v_G=\lambda(1,1,1)$. Then $v_F=v-v_G=(3-\lambda,1-\lambda,2-\lambda)$. We need $v_F\in F$:
$$(3-\lambda)+(1-\lambda)+(2-\lambda)=0 \implies 6-3\lambda=0 \implies \lambda=2.$$

**Decomposition.** $v_G=2(1,1,1)=(2,2,2)$ and $v_F=(3,1,2)-(2,2,2)=(1,-1,0)$.

**Verification.** $(1,-1,0)\in F$ since $1-1+0=0$. And $v=v_F+v_G$.

**Conclusion.** $v=(1,-1,0)+(2,2,2)$.

@@@ vector-sum-direct-criterion.md
---
title: The direct sum criterion
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: direct sum, criterion, proof
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $F$ and $G$ be subspaces of a finite-dimensional vector space $E$. Show that $F+G$ is a direct sum if and only if $\dim(F+G)=\dim F+\dim G$.

## Hint

Use the dimension formula $\dim(F+G)=\dim F+\dim G-\dim(F\cap G)$.

## Solution

**If $F+G$ is direct.** Then $F\cap G=\{0\}$, so $\dim(F\cap G)=0$. The dimension formula gives $\dim(F+G)=\dim F+\dim G$.

**Conversely.** If $\dim(F+G)=\dim F+\dim G$, the dimension formula gives $\dim(F\cap G)=0$, so $F\cap G=\{0\}$. This is exactly the definition of a direct sum.

**Conclusion.** $F+G$ is direct if and only if the dimensions add.

@@@ vector-kernel-image-linear-map.md
---
title: Kernel and image of a linear map
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: kernel, image, linear map, rank-nullity
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^3\to\mathbb{R}^3$ be defined by
$$f(x,y,z)=(x+y, y+z, x+2y+z).$$
Compute $\ker f$, $\operatorname{Im} f$, and check the rank-nullity theorem.

## Hint

Write the matrix of $f$ and compute its rank by row reduction.

## Solution

**Matrix.**
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Row reduction.** $L_3\leftarrow L_3-L_1$:
$$\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$
$L_3\leftarrow L_3-L_2$:
$$\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Rank is $2$.

**Kernel.** Solve $Ax=0$: $x+y=0$ and $y+z=0$, so $y=-x$ and $z=x$. Kernel is $\operatorname{span}((1,-1,1))$. Dimension $1$.

**Image.** Spanned by the first two columns: $(1,0,1)$ and $(1,1,2)$. Dimension $2$.

**Rank-nullity check.** $2+1=3=\dim\mathbb{R}^3$.

**Conclusion.** $\ker f=\operatorname{span}((1,-1,1))$ and $\operatorname{Im} f=\operatorname{span}((1,0,1),(1,1,2))$.

@@@ vector-matrix-rank-nullity.md
---
title: Rank-nullity on a 3Ã—4 matrix
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: rank-nullity, rank, matrix
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 \\ 2 & 4 & 6 & 9 \\ 1 & 2 & 3 & 5 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Compute the rank of $A$ and the dimension of its kernel.

## Hint

Row-reduce $A$ and use rank-nullity with $n=4$.

## Solution

**Row reduction.** $L_2\leftarrow L_2-2L_1$, $L_3\leftarrow L_3-L_1$:
$$\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$
$L_3\leftarrow L_3-L_2$:
$$\begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}.$$
Rank is $2$.

**Kernel dimension.** By rank-nullity, $\dim\ker A = 4-2 = 2$.

**Kernel.** Solve $Ax=0$: $x_1+2x_2+3x_3+4x_4=0$ and $x_4=0$. So $x_1+2x_2+3x_3=0$. Free variables $x_2, x_3$; basis $(-2,1,0,0)$, $(-3,0,1,0)$.

**Conclusion.** $\operatorname{rank}(A)=2$ and $\dim(\ker A)=2$.

@@@ vector-change-of-basis.md
---
title: Change of basis in a polynomial space
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: change of basis, transition matrix, polynomials
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $\mathcal{B}=(1,X,X^2)$ and $\mathcal{B}'=(1,X-1,(X-1)^2)$ be two bases of $\mathbb{R}_2[X]$. Compute the transition matrix from $\mathcal{B}$ to $\mathcal{B}'$, and the coordinates of $P(X)=2X^2-3X+1$ in $\mathcal{B}'$.

## Hint

Express each vector of $\mathcal{B}'$ in terms of $\mathcal{B}$ to build $P_{\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}'}$. Then use $[P]_{\mathcal{B}'} = P_{\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}'}^{-1}[P]_{\mathcal{B}}$.

## Solution

**Transition matrix.** In basis $\mathcal{B}$:
$$1=(1,0,0), \quad X-1=(-1,1,0), \quad (X-1)^2=(1,-2,1).$$
So
$$P_{\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}'} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & -1 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 & -2 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Inverse.** Since $P$ is triangular with diagonal $1$, its inverse is
$$P^{-1} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 & 2 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Coordinates in $\mathcal{B}'$.** $[P]_{\mathcal{B}}=(1,-3,2)$. Then
$$[P]_{\mathcal{B}'} = P^{-1}\begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ -3 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 1-3+2 \\ -3+4 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \\ 2 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Verification.** $P=0\cdot 1+1\cdot(X-1)+2\cdot(X-1)^2 = X-1+2(X^2-2X+1) = 2X^2-3X+1$. Correct.

@@@ vector-orthogonal-complement.md
---
title: Orthogonal complement
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: orthogonal complement, dimension, inner product
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

In $\mathbb{R}^3$ with the standard inner product, let $F=\operatorname{span}((1,1,0),(0,1,1))$. Compute $F^\perp$ and verify $\dim F+\dim F^\perp=3$.

## Hint

$F^\perp$ is the set of vectors orthogonal to a generating set of $F$.

## Solution

**Equations.** $v=(x,y,z)\in F^\perp$ iff $v\cdot(1,1,0)=0$ and $v\cdot(0,1,1)=0$:
$$x+y=0, \qquad y+z=0.$$
So $y=-x$ and $z=-y=x$. Thus $v=x(1,-1,1)$.

**Conclusion.** $F^\perp=\operatorname{span}((1,-1,1))$, dimension $1$.

**Check.** $\dim F=2$ (the two generators are independent) and $\dim F^\perp=1$, so $2+1=3=\dim\mathbb{R}^3$. This confirms $\mathbb{R}^3=F\oplus F^\perp$.

@@@ vector-gram-schmidt-subspace.md
---
title: Orthonormal basis of a subspace
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Gram-Schmidt, orthonormal basis, subspace
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find an orthonormal basis of $F=\operatorname{span}((1,1,0),(1,0,1))$ in $\mathbb{R}^3$.

## Hint

Apply Gram-Schmidt to the two generators.

## Solution

**Step 1.** $u_1=(1,1,0)/\sqrt2$.

**Step 2.** Projection of $(1,0,1)$ onto $u_1$: $\langle(1,0,1),u_1\rangle = 1/\sqrt2$. Orthogonal part:
$$(1,0,1) - \frac{1}{\sqrt2}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt2}(1,1,0) = (1,0,1)-(1/2,1/2,0)=(1/2,-1/2,1).$$
Norm: $\sqrt{1/4+1/4+1}=\sqrt{3/2}$. So
$$u_2 = \sqrt{2/3}(1/2,-1/2,1) = (1/\sqrt6,-1/\sqrt6,\sqrt{2/3}).$$

**Conclusion.** An orthonormal basis of $F$ is
$$u_1=\frac1{\sqrt2}(1,1,0), \qquad u_2=\frac1{\sqrt6}(1,-1,2).$$

@@@ vector-quotient-space.md
---
title: Quotient vector space
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: quotient space, cosets, dimension
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $E=\mathbb{R}^3$ and $F=\operatorname{span}((1,1,1))$. Describe the quotient space $E/F$ and compute its dimension.

## Hint

Two vectors are equivalent mod $F$ iff their difference is a multiple of $(1,1,1)$.

## Solution

**Equivalence.** $u\sim v$ iff $u-v\in F$, i.e. $u-v=\lambda(1,1,1)$ for some $\lambda\in\mathbb{R}$. So $u$ and $v$ have the same "difference of coordinates": if $u=(x_1,y_1,z_1)$ and $v=(x_2,y_2,z_2)$, then $u\sim v$ iff $x_1-x_2=y_1-y_2=z_1-z_2$.

**Classes.** Each class can be represented by a vector of the form $(a,b,0)$, where $a=x-z$ and $b=y-z$. The map $E/F\to\mathbb{R}^2$, $[x,y,z]\mapsto(x-z,y-z)$, is an isomorphism.

**Dimension.** $\dim(E/F)=\dim E-\dim F=3-1=2$.

**Conclusion.** $E/F\cong\mathbb{R}^2$.

@@@ vector-isomorphism-criterion.md
---
title: Isomorphism criterion
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: isomorphism, dimension, injective linear map
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $E$ and $F$ be finite-dimensional vector spaces with $\dim E=\dim F=n$, and let $f:E\to F$ be linear. Show that $f$ is an isomorphism if and only if $\ker f=\{0\}$, if and only if $\operatorname{Im} f=F$.

## Hint

Use the rank-nullity theorem: $\dim\ker f+\dim\operatorname{Im} f=n$.

## Solution

**Injectivity.** $\ker f=\{0\}$ iff $\dim\ker f=0$ iff (by rank-nullity) $\dim\operatorname{Im} f=n=\dim F$ iff $\operatorname{Im} f=F$ iff $f$ is surjective.

**Conclusion.** For a linear map between spaces of the same finite dimension, injective $\Leftrightarrow$ surjective $\Leftrightarrow$ bijective $\Leftrightarrow$ isomorphism. This fails in infinite dimension (e.g. the shift operator on sequences).

@@@ vector-basis-image.md

title: Image of a basis under a linear map
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: basis, image, linear map
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^2\to\mathbb{R}^3$ be the linear map defined by $f(1,0)=(1,2,3)$ and $f(0,1)=(0,1,1)$. Compute a basis and the dimension of $\operatorname{Im} f$.

## Hint

The image of a basis spans the image. Then reduce to a basis.

## Solution

**Generators.** $\operatorname{Im} f=\operatorname{span}((1,2,3),(0,1,1))$.

**Independence.** The two vectors are not proportional, so they are independent.

**Conclusion.** A basis of $\operatorname{Im} f$ is $((1,2,3),(0,1,1))$, and $\dim\operatorname{Im} f=2$.

**Remark.** $\operatorname{Im} f$ is a plane in $\mathbb{R}^3$.

@@@ vector-kernel-basis.md

title: Basis of a kernel

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^3\to\mathbb{R}$ be the linear form $f(x,y,z)=x+2y+3z$. Find a basis of $\ker f$.

## Hint

The kernel is a plane. Solve $x+2y+3z=0$ and express the solutions in terms of two free parameters.

## Solution

**Equation.** $x=-2y-3z$. The solutions are
$$(x,y,z)=(-2y-3z,y,z)=y(-2,1,0)+z(-3,0,1).$$

**Dimension.** $\dim\ker f=2$.
