@@@ eigenvalues-diagonalization-criterion-minimal.md
---
title: Diagonalizability and the minimal polynomial
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: minimal polynomial, diagonalization, criterion
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be an $n\times n$ matrix. Show that $A$ is diagonalizable if and only if its minimal polynomial has only simple roots.

## Hint

If $A$ is diagonalizable, the minimal polynomial is $\prod(X-\lambda_i)$ over distinct eigenvalues. Conversely, if the minimal polynomial has simple roots, use the kernel decomposition.

## Solution

**Forward direction.** If $A=PDP^{-1}$ with $D=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_n)$, then $A$ is killed by $\prod_i(X-\lambda_i)$ where the product is over distinct eigenvalues. The minimal polynomial is the smallest such polynomial, so it is a product of distinct linear factors.

**Converse.** Let $\mu_A(X)=\prod_{i=1}^r(X-\lambda_i)$ with distinct $\lambda_i$. For each $i$, let $E_i=\ker(A-\lambda_i I)$. We claim that the sum $E_1+\cdots+E_r$ is $\mathbb{R}^n$.

Indeed, the polynomials $P_i(X)=\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{X-\lambda_j}{\lambda_i-\lambda_j}$ form a Lagrange interpolation basis: $\sum_i P_i=1$ and $P_i$ is zero at all $\lambda_j$ for $j\neq i$, and $P_i(\lambda_i)=1$.

For any $v\in\mathbb{R}^n$: $v=\sum_i P_i(A)v$ (since $\sum_i P_i=1$). But $(A-\lambda_i I)P_i(A)v = \mu_A(A)\cdot(\text{something})=0$, so $P_i(A)v\in E_i$. Hence $v$ decomposes as a sum of eigenvectors, and $\mathbb{R}^n$ is spanned by the eigenspaces. Thus $A$ is diagonalizable.

**Conclusion.** $A$ is diagonalizable iff its minimal polynomial has only simple roots.

@@@ eigenvalues-circulant.md
---
title: Eigenvalues of a circulant matrix
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: circulant matrix, eigenvalues, roots of unity
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let
$$C = \begin{pmatrix} a & b & c \\ c & a & b \\ b & c & a \end{pmatrix}.$$
Show that the eigenvalues of $C$ are
$$\lambda_k = a + b\omega^k + c\omega^{2k}, \qquad k=0,1,2,$$
where $\omega=e^{2i\pi/3}$, with eigenvectors $(1,\omega^k,\omega^{2k})$.

## Hint

Compute $C v$ for $v=(1,\omega,\omega^2)$ and use $\omega^3=1$, $1+\omega+\omega^2=0$.

## Solution

**Computation.** Let $v_k=(1,\omega^k,\omega^{2k})^T$. Then
$$(Cv_k)_1 = a\cdot1+b\cdot\omega^k+c\cdot\omega^{2k} = \lambda_k.$$
$$(Cv_k)_2 = c\cdot1+a\cdot\omega^k+b\cdot\omega^{2k} = \omega^k(a+b\omega^k+c\omega^{2k}) = \omega^k\lambda_k.$$
$$(Cv_k)_3 = b\cdot1+c\cdot\omega^k+a\cdot\omega^{2k} = \omega^{2k}(a+b\omega^k+c\omega^{2k}) = \omega^{2k}\lambda_k.$$
So $Cv_k=\lambda_k v_k$, and $\lambda_k=a+b\omega^k+c\omega^{2k}$.

**Diagonalizability.** For $k=0,1,2$, the vectors $v_k$ are linearly independent (Vandermonde-type argument with distinct $\omega^k$). So $C$ is diagonalizable over $\mathbb{C}$, with eigenvalues $\lambda_0,\lambda_1,\lambda_2$.

**Conclusion.** The eigenvalues of the circulant matrix $C$ are given by the discrete Fourier transform of $(a,b,c)$.

@@@ eigenvalues-diagonal-conjugation.md
---
title: Similar matrices have the same eigenvalues
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: similarity, eigenvalues, characteristic polynomial
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ and $B=P^{-1}AP$ be similar matrices. Show that $A$ and $B$ have the same characteristic polynomial, hence the same eigenvalues with the same algebraic multiplicities.

## Hint

Compute $\det(B-\lambda I)$ by factoring $P^{-1}$ and $P$.

## Solution

**Characteristic polynomial.**
$$\det(B-\lambda I)=\det(P^{-1}AP-\lambda I)=\det(P^{-1}(A-\lambda I)P)=\det(P^{-1})\det(A-\lambda I)\det(P).$$
Since $\det(P^{-1})\det(P)=1$, we get $\det(B-\lambda I)=\det(A-\lambda I)$.

**Consequence.** $\chi_A=\chi_B$, so $A$ and $B$ have the same eigenvalues with the same multiplicities.

**Eigenvectors.** If $Av=\lambda v$, then $B(P^{-1}v)=P^{-1}Av=\lambda P^{-1}v$. So $P^{-1}$ maps eigenspaces of $A$ to eigenspaces of $B$. In particular, $\dim E_\lambda(A)=\dim E_\lambda(B)$.

**Conclusion.** Similar matrices have identical spectral data.

@@@ eigenvalues-diagonal-powers.md
---
title: Powers via diagonalization
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: diagonalization, powers, matrix
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A=PDP^{-1}$ with $D=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_n)$. Show that for all $k\ge1$,
$$A^k=PD^kP^{-1}=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1^k,\ldots,\lambda_n^k)$$
in the appropriate basis.

## Hint

Prove by induction using $A=PDP^{-1}$.

## Solution

**Induction.** For $k=1$, $A^1=PDP^{-1}$. Suppose $A^k=PD^kP^{-1}$. Then
$$A^{k+1}=A^k\cdot A=PD^kP^{-1}\cdot PDP^{-1}=PD^kDP^{-1}=PD^{k+1}P^{-1}.$$

**Diagonal power.** $D^k=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1^k,\ldots,\lambda_n^k)$.

**Conclusion.** $A^k=PD^kP^{-1}$. This formula is used to compute powers efficiently once $A$ is diagonalized.

**Application.** If $D=\operatorname{diag}(2,3)$ and $P=I$, then $A^k=\operatorname{diag}(2^k,3^k)$.

@@@ eigenvalues-spectral-decomposition.md
---
title: Spectral decomposition of a symmetric matrix
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: spectral decomposition, symmetric matrix, projection
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be a real symmetric matrix with distinct eigenvalues $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_r$. Let $P_i$ be the orthogonal projection onto the eigenspace $E_{\lambda_i}$. Show that
$$A=\sum_{i=1}^r\lambda_i P_i, \qquad \sum_{i=1}^r P_i = I.$$

## Hint

In an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors, $A$ is diagonal with eigenvalues on the diagonal; interpret the diagonal entries via the projections.

## Solution

**Orthonormal eigenbasis.** Since $A$ is symmetric, there is an orthonormal basis $(u_1,\ldots,u_n)$ of eigenvectors, with $Au_j=\mu_j u_j$ (where $\mu_j$ ranges over the $\lambda_i$).

**Projections.** $P_i$ is the orthogonal projection onto $E_{\lambda_i}$: for any $v$, $P_i v$ is the sum of the components of $v$ along eigenvectors with eigenvalue $\lambda_i$.

**Spectral decomposition.** For any $v$:
$$Av=\sum_j \mu_j\langle v,u_j\rangle u_j=\sum_i \lambda_i\sum_{j:\mu_j=\lambda_i}\langle v,u_j\rangle u_j=\sum_i\lambda_i P_i v.$$
So $A=\sum_i\lambda_i P_i$.

**Completeness.** $\sum_i P_i$ projects onto the sum of the eigenspaces, which is $\mathbb{R}^n$, so $\sum_i P_i=I$.

**Conclusion.** Symmetric matrices admit a spectral decomposition as a sum of orthogonal projections scaled by eigenvalues.

@@@ eigenvalues-diagonal-functions.md
---
title: Functions of a diagonalizable matrix
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: functions of matrices, diagonalization, polynomial
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A=PDP^{-1}$ with $D=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_n)$. Define $f(A)=P f(D) P^{-1}$ where $f(D)=\operatorname{diag}(f(\lambda_1),\ldots,f(\lambda_n))$ for a function $f$ defined on the eigenvalues. Justify this definition for polynomials $f$.

## Hint

For a polynomial $f(X)=\sum_k a_k X^k$, $f(A)=\sum_k a_k A^k$.

## Solution

**Polynomial case.** For $f(X)=\sum_k a_k X^k$:
$$f(A)=\sum_k a_k A^k=\sum_k a_k PD^kP^{-1}=P\left(\sum_k a_k D^k\right)P^{-1}=Pf(D)P^{-1}.$$
So the definition is consistent with the polynomial case.

**Extension.** For other functions (e.g. $\exp$, $\sin$, $\log$), the same formula defines $f(A)$ provided $f$ is defined on the spectrum of $A$. This requires $A$ to be diagonalizable.

**Example.** $e^{tA}=P\operatorname{diag}(e^{t\lambda_1},\ldots,e^{t\lambda_n})P^{-1}$.

**Conclusion.** Functions of a diagonalizable matrix are defined via the diagonal form. For non-diagonalizable matrices, one uses Taylor or Jordan form instead.

@@@ determinant-2x2-basic.md
---
title: Determinant of a 2Ã—2 matrix
category: linear/determinants
level: lycee
difficulty: 1
tags: determinant, 2x2 matrices, invertibility
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the determinant of each matrix and decide whether it is invertible:

(a) $A = \begin{pmatrix} 3 & 1 \\ 5 & 2 \end{pmatrix}$;
(b) $B = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 \\ 2 & 4 \end{pmatrix}$;
(c) $C = \begin{pmatrix} \cos\theta & -\sin\theta \\ \sin\theta & \cos\theta \end{pmatrix}$.

## Hint

For a $2\times2$ matrix $\begin{pmatrix} a & b \\ c & d \end{pmatrix}$, the determinant is $ad-bc$. The matrix is invertible iff the determinant is nonzero.

## Solution

**(a)** $\det A = 3\cdot2 - 1\cdot5 = 6-5=1$. Invertible.

**(b)** $\det B = 1\cdot4 - 2\cdot2 = 0$. Not invertible.

**(c)** $\det C = \cos\theta\cdot\cos\theta - (-\sin\theta)(\sin\theta) = \cos^2\theta+\sin^2\theta = 1$. Invertible for all $\theta$ (it is a rotation matrix).

@@@ determinant-3x3-sarrus.md
---
title: Determinant of a 3Ã—3 matrix by Sarrus' rule
category: linear/determinants
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: determinant, Sarrus rule, 3x3 matrices
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the determinant of
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 \\ 4 & 5 & 6 \\ 7 & 8 & 10 \end{pmatrix}.$$

## Hint

Use Sarrus' rule: add the three products along downward diagonals and subtract the three products along upward diagonals.

## Solution

**Sarrus' rule.**
$$\det A = 1\cdot5\cdot10 + 2\cdot6\cdot7 + 3\cdot4\cdot8 - 3\cdot5\cdot7 - 1\cdot6\cdot8 - 2\cdot4\cdot10.$$
Compute each term:
$$= 50 + 84 + 96 - 105 - 48 - 80 = 230 - 233 = -3.$$

**Conclusion.** $\det A = -3$, so $A$ is invertible.

@@@ determinant-expansion-row.md

title: Expansion along a row
category: linear/determinants
level: lycee
difficulty: 3
tags: cofactor expansion, minors, determinant
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the determinant of
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 3 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 4 & 0 \\ 5 & 0 & 0 & 6 \end{pmatrix}$$
by expanding along the most convenient row or column.

## Hint

Expand along the second row, which has only one nonzero entry.

## Solution

**Expansion along the second row.** The only nonzero entry is $a_{22}=3$. Its cofactor is $(-1)^{2+2}=+1$ times the determinant of the $3\times3$ minor obtained by deleting row $2$ and column $2$:
$$M_{22} = \begin{vmatrix} 2 & 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 4 & 0 \\ 5 & 0 & 6 \end{vmatrix}.$$

**Expand $M_{22}$ along its second row.** The only nonzero entry is $4$ at position $(2,2)$, cofactor $(+1)\begin{vmatrix}2&1\\5&6\end{vmatrix} = 12-5=7$.

So $M_{22} = 4\cdot7 = 28$.

**Conclusion.** $\det A = 3\cdot28 = 84$.

**Check by permutation formula.** The matrix is a block "diagonal with a corner" structure; the result $2\cdot3\cdot4\cdot6 + (\text{sign})\cdot1\cdot3\cdot4\cdot5 = 144 - 60 = 84$ confirms it.

@@@ determinant-expansion-column.md
---
title: Expansion along a column
category: linear/determinants
level: lycee
difficulty: 3
tags: cofactor expansion, minors, determinant
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the determinant of
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 \\ 0 & 4 & 5 \\ 1 & 0 & 6 \end{pmatrix}$$
by expanding along the first column.

## Hint

Use the cofactor expansion along column $1$: $\det A = \sum_i (-1)^{i+1}a_{i1}\det M_{i1}$.

## Solution

**Expansion along column 1.**
$$\det A = 1\cdot\begin{vmatrix}4&5\\0&6\end{vmatrix} - 0\cdot\begin{vmatrix}2&3\\0&6\end{vmatrix} + 1\cdot\begin{vmatrix}2&3\\4&5\end{vmatrix}.$$

**Minor computations.**
$$\begin{vmatrix}4&5\\0&6\end{vmatrix} = 24, \qquad \begin{vmatrix}2&3\\4&5\end{vmatrix} = 10-12=-2.$$

**Conclusion.** $\det A = 1\cdot24 + 1\cdot(-2) = 22$.

**Check by Sarrus.** $1\cdot4\cdot6 + 2\cdot5\cdot1 + 3\cdot0\cdot0 - 3\cdot4\cdot1 - 2\cdot0\cdot6 - 1\cdot5\cdot0 = 24+10+0-12-0-0 = 22$. Consistent.

@@@ determinant-row-operation-effect.md
---
title: Effect of row operations on the determinant
category: linear/determinants
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: row operations, determinant properties, multilinearity
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Describe the effect of each elementary row operation on the determinant:

1. $L_i \leftrightarrow L_j$ (swap two rows);
2. $L_i \leftarrow \lambda L_i$ with $\lambda\neq 0$ (multiply a row by $\lambda$);
3. $L_i \leftarrow L_i + \lambda L_j$ with $j\neq i$ (add a multiple of another row).

## Hint

Use multilinearity and the alternating property of the determinant.

## Solution

**1. Swapping.** The determinant changes sign: $\det = -\det_{\text{original}}$. This follows from the alternating property (the determinant is zero when two rows are equal, and swapping is an odd permutation).

**2. Scaling.** Multiplying a row by $\lambda$ multiplies the determinant by $\lambda$: $\det = \lambda\cdot\det_{\text{original}}$. This follows from linearity in each row.

**3. Adding a multiple.** Adding a multiple of one row to another leaves the determinant unchanged. Indeed, writing the new row as $L_i+\lambda L_j$, linearity gives $\det(L_i+\lambda L_j,\ldots)=\det(L_i,\ldots)+\lambda\det(L_j,\ldots)$, and the second determinant has two equal rows, so it is zero.

**Conclusion.** Swapping changes sign; scaling by $\lambda$ scales by $\lambda$; adding a multiple leaves the determinant unchanged. These rules are used to simplify determinant computations.

@@@ determinant-triangular.md
---
title: Determinant of a triangular matrix
category: linear/determinants
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: triangular matrix, determinant, diagonal
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that the determinant of an upper triangular (or lower triangular) matrix is the product of its diagonal entries.

## Hint

Expand recursively along the first column (or last row).

## Solution

**Expansion along the first column.** Let $T$ be upper triangular with diagonal entries $t_{11},\ldots,t_{nn}$. In the first column, only $t_{11}$ is nonzero, so
$$\det T = t_{11}\det T_{11},$$
where $T_{11}$ is the $(n-1)\times(n-1)$ upper triangular submatrix obtained by removing the first row and first column, with diagonal entries $t_{22},\ldots,t_{nn}$.

**Induction.** Repeating the expansion, we get $\det T = t_{11}t_{22}\cdots t_{nn}$.

**Lower triangular case.** The same argument works by expanding along the first row (or by transposing: $\det T = \det T^T$, and $T^T$ is upper triangular).

**Conclusion.** For any triangular matrix, the determinant equals the product of the diagonal entries.

@@@ determinant-multilinearity.md
---
title: Multilinearity of the determinant
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: multilinearity, determinant properties, proof
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be an $n\times n$ matrix. Show that the determinant is linear in each column: if $C_j = \lambda C_j' + \mu C_j''$, then
$$\det(A) = \lambda\det(A')+\mu\det(A''),$$
where $A'$ and $A''$ are obtained from $A$ by replacing column $j$ with $C_j'$ and $C_j''$ respectively, the other columns being unchanged.

## Hint

Use the Leibniz formula: $\det A = \sum_{\sigma\in S_n}\varepsilon(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^n a_{\sigma(i),i}$, which is linear in each column's entries.

## Solution

**Leibniz formula.** For an $n\times n$ matrix with columns $C_1,\ldots,C_n$, each entry $a_{ij}$ is the $i$-th entry of $C_j$. The Leibniz formula is
$$\det A = \sum_{\sigma\in S_n}\varepsilon(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^n a_{\sigma(i),i}.$$

**Linearity in column $j$.** In this formula, column $j$ appears only through the factor $a_{\sigma(j),j}$ (the entry in row $\sigma(j)$ and column $j$). Since $a_{\sigma(j),j}=\lambda a'_{\sigma(j),j}+\mu a''_{\sigma(j),j}$, the product is linear in that entry, hence the entire sum is linear in column $j$.

**Conclusion.** The determinant is linear in each column separately (multilinear). Combined with the alternating property (zero when two columns are equal, sign change under transposition), this characterises the determinant.

@@@ determinant-alternating.md
---
title: The determinant is alternating
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: alternating, equal columns, sign change
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that:
1. If two columns of $A$ are equal, then $\det A = 0$;
2. Swapping two columns changes the sign of the determinant.

## Hint

For (1), use the Leibniz formula and pair permutations with their transpositions. For (2), use multilinearity and (1).

## Solution

**1. Equal columns.** Suppose columns $j$ and $k$ (with $j\neq k$) are equal. Consider the transposition $\tau=(j\,k)$ in $S_n$. For each permutation $\sigma$, $\sigma\circ\tau$ has the same sign as $\sigma$ (since $\varepsilon(\tau)=-1$ and $\varepsilon(\sigma\circ\tau)=-\varepsilon(\sigma)$). Using the Leibniz formula, pair up the terms:
$$\det A = \sum_\sigma \varepsilon(\sigma)\prod_i a_{\sigma(i),i} = \sum_\sigma \varepsilon(\sigma\circ\tau)\prod_i a_{\sigma\circ\tau(i),i}.$$
But because columns $j$ and $k$ are equal, $\prod_i a_{\sigma\circ\tau(i),i} = \prod_i a_{\sigma(i),i}$. So the two sums cancel, giving $\det A = -\det A$, hence $\det A=0$.

**2. Swapping columns.** Let $B$ be obtained from $A$ by swapping columns $j$ and $k$. Consider the determinant of $A+B$: column $j$ of $A+B$ is $C_j + C_k$, and column $k$ is $C_k+C_j$, so these columns are equal. By (1), $\det(A+B)=0$. By multilinearity:
$$0 = \det(A+B) = \det A + \det A^{j\leftarrow k} + \det A^{k\leftarrow j} + \det B,$$
where $A^{j\leftarrow k}$ has column $j$ replaced by $C_k$ (and column $k$ unchanged) â€” this matrix has two equal columns $C_k$, so its determinant is zero. Similarly $\det A^{k\leftarrow j}=0$. Hence $\det B = -\det A$.

**Conclusion.** The determinant is alternating: zero on equal columns, and swapping two columns flips the sign.

@@@ determinant-transpose.md
---
title: Determinant of the transpose
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: transpose, determinant, proof
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $\det(A^T)=\det(A)$ for every square matrix $A$.

## Hint

Use the Leibniz formula on both sides and reindex the permutations.

## Solution

**Leibniz formula.** 
$$\det A = \sum_{\sigma\in S_n}\varepsilon(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^n a_{\sigma(i),i}.$$

**Transpose.** The entry $(i,j)$ of $A^T$ is $a_{ji}$. So
$$\det(A^T) = \sum_{\sigma\in S_n}\varepsilon(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^n a_{i,\sigma(i)}.$$
Since $\prod_{i=1}^n a_{i,\sigma(i)}=\prod_{j=1}^n a_{\sigma^{-1}(j),j}$ (reindexing $j=\sigma(i)$), and $\varepsilon(\sigma^{-1})=\varepsilon(\sigma)$ (since $\varepsilon$ is a group homomorphism and $\sigma\sigma^{-1}=e$), we can rewrite:
$$\det(A^T) = \sum_{\sigma}\varepsilon(\sigma^{-1})\prod_j a_{\sigma^{-1}(j),j} = \sum_{\tau}\varepsilon(\tau)\prod_j a_{\tau(j),j} = \det A,$$
where the last change of variables is $\tau=\sigma^{-1}$.

**Conclusion.** $\det(A^T)=\det A$. In particular, properties of columns hold for rows too.

@@@ determinant-product.md
---
title: Determinant of a product
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: determinant of product, multiplicativity, proof
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $\det(AB)=\det(A)\det(B)$ for $A,B\in\mathcal{M}_n(\mathbb{R})$.

## Hint

Consider the map $B\mapsto\det(AB)$ as a function of the columns of $B$, and show it is alternating multilinear.

## Solution

**Key idea.** The map $\varphi:B\mapsto\det(AB)$ is multilinear and alternating in the columns of $B$, because $AB$ is linear in the columns of $B$, and the determinant is multilinear and alternating. Moreover, $\varphi(I)=\det(A)$.

**Uniqueness.** Any alternating multilinear form on $\mathcal{M}_n(\mathbb{R})$ is a scalar multiple of the determinant. So there exists a constant $c$ with $\varphi(B)=c\det(B)$ for all $B$. Setting $B=I$: $\varphi(I)=c\det I=c$, so $c=\det A$.

**Conclusion.** $\det(AB)=\det A\cdot\det B$ for all $A,B$.

**Corollary.** If $A$ is invertible, $\det(A^{-1})=1/\det A$. If $A$ is invertible and $B=P^{-1}AP$, then $\det B=\det A$.

@@@ determinant-inverse.md
---
title: Determinant of an inverse
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: inverse, determinant, formula
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be an invertible matrix. Show that $\det(A^{-1})=1/\det A$.

## Hint

Use $AA^{-1}=I$ and multiplicativity of the determinant.

## Solution

**Computation.** $AA^{-1}=I$, so
$$\det(A)\det(A^{-1}) = \det(AA^{-1})=\det I = 1.$$
Since $A$ is invertible, $\det A\neq0$, so $\det(A^{-1})=1/\det A$.

**Conclusion.** The determinant of the inverse is the inverse of the determinant.

@@@ determinant-cramers-rule-2x2.md
---
title: Cramer's rule for a 2Ã—2 system
category: linear/determinants
level: lycee
difficulty: 3
tags: Cramer's rule, linear system, determinant
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve using Cramer's rule:
$$\begin{cases} 3x + 2y = 7 \\ x - y = 1 \end{cases}$$

## Hint

For $AX=B$ with $\det A\neq0$, $x_i=\det A_i/\det A$, where $A_i$ is $A$ with column $i$ replaced by $B$.

## Solution

**Determinant.** 
$$\det A = \begin{vmatrix} 3 & 2 \\ 1 & -1 \end{vmatrix} = -3-2=-5\neq0.$$

**Determinant $A_1$.**
$$\det A_1 = \begin{vmatrix} 7 & 2 \\ 1 & -1 \end{vmatrix} = -7-2=-9.$$

**Determinant $A_2$.**
$$\det A_2 = \begin{vmatrix} 3 & 7 \\ 1 & 1 \end{vmatrix} = 3-7=-4.$$

**Solution.**
$$x = \frac{-9}{-5} = \frac95, \qquad y = \frac{-4}{-5}=\frac45.$$

**Verification.** $3\cdot9/5+2\cdot4/5=(27+8)/5=35/5=7$ and $9/5-4/5=5/5=1$. Correct.

@@@ determinant-cramers-rule-3x3.md
---
title: Cramer's rule for a 3Ã—3 system
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Cramer's rule, 3x3 determinant, linear system
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve using Cramer's rule:
$$\begin{cases} x + y + z = 6 \\ 2x - y + z = 3 \\ x + 2y - z = 2 \end{cases}$$

## Hint

Compute four $3\times3$ determinants.

## Solution

**Determinant of $A$.**
$$\det A = \begin{vmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 2 & -1 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 & -1 \end{vmatrix} = 1(1-2)-1(-2-1)+1(4+1)=-1+3+5=7.$$

**Determinant $A_1$** (replace column 1 by $B=(6,3,2)^T$):
$$\det A_1 = \begin{vmatrix} 6 & 1 & 1 \\ 3 & -1 & 1 \\ 2 & 2 & -1 \end{vmatrix} = 6(1-2)-1(-3-2)+1(6+2)=-6+5+8=7.$$

**Determinant $A_2$** (replace column 2 by $B$):
$$\det A_2 = \begin{vmatrix} 1 & 6 & 1 \\ 2 & 3 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 & -1 \end{vmatrix} = 1(-3-2)-6(-2-1)+1(4-3)=-5+18+1=14.$$

**Determinant $A_3$** (replace column 3 by $B$):
$$\det A_3 = \begin{vmatrix} 1 & 1 & 6 \\ 2 & -1 & 3 \\ 1 & 2 & 2 \end{vmatrix} = 1(-2-6)-1(4-3)+6(4+1)=-8-1+30=21.$$

**Solution.**
$$x=\frac77=1, \qquad y=\frac{14}{7}=2, \qquad z=\frac{21}{7}=3.$$

**Verification.** $1+2+3=6$, $2-2+3=3$, $1+4-3=2$. Correct.

@@@ determinant-cofactor-formula.md
---
title: The cofactor formula for the inverse
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: cofactor, comatrix, inverse formula
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be invertible. Define the cofactor $C_{ij}=(-1)^{i+j}\det M_{ij}$, where $M_{ij}$ is the minor obtained by deleting row $i$ and column $j$. Define the comatrix $\operatorname{com}(A)=(C_{ij})^T$ (transpose of the cofactor matrix). Show that
$$A^{-1}=\frac{1}{\det A}\operatorname{com}(A).$$

## Hint

Show that $A\cdot\operatorname{com}(A)=\det(A)\cdot I$, using the expansion of the determinant along a row.

## Solution

**Entry $(i,j)$ of $A\cdot\operatorname{com}(A)$.** 
$$(A\cdot\operatorname{com}(A))_{ij}=\sum_k a_{ik}C_{jk}.$$

**Case $i=j$.** $\sum_k a_{ik}C_{ik}=\det A$ by expansion along row $i$ (each term is a cofactor times its entry).

**Case $i\neq j$.** $\sum_k a_{ik}C_{jk}$ is the determinant of the matrix obtained from $A$ by replacing row $j$ with row $i$. This matrix has two equal rows, so its determinant is zero.

**Conclusion.** $A\cdot\operatorname{com}(A)=\det(A)\cdot I$, so $A^{-1}=\operatorname{com}(A)/\det A$.

@@@ determinant-cofactor-computation.md
---
title: Computing the inverse with cofactors
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: cofactor, comatrix, inverse
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the inverse of
$$A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 \\ 0 & 1 & 4 \\ 5 & 6 & 0 \end{pmatrix}$$
using the cofactor formula.

## Hint

Compute all $3\times3$ cofactors, transpose, and divide by the determinant.

## Solution

**Determinant.** $\det A = 1(0-24) - 2(0-20) + 3(0-5) = -24 + 40 - 15 = 1$.

**Cofactors.**
- $C_{11}=+(1\cdot0 - 4\cdot6) = -24$.
- $C_{12}=-(0\cdot0-4\cdot5)=20$.
- $C_{13}=+(0\cdot6-1\cdot5)=-5$.
- $C_{21}=-(2\cdot0-3\cdot6)=18$.
- $C_{22}=+(1\cdot0-3\cdot5)=-15$.
- $C_{23}=-(1\cdot6-2\cdot5)=4$.
- $C_{31}=+(2\cdot4-3\cdot1)=5$.
- $C_{32}=-(1\cdot4-3\cdot0)=-4$.
- $C_{33}=+(1\cdot1-2\cdot0)=1$.

**Cofactor matrix and transpose.**
$$C = \begin{pmatrix} -24 & 20 & -5 \\ 18 & -15 & 4 \\ 5 & -4 & 1 \end{pmatrix}, \qquad \operatorname{com}(A) = C^T = \begin{pmatrix} -24 & 18 & 5 \\ 20 & -15 & -4 \\ -5 & 4 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Inverse.** Since $\det A = 1$,
$$A^{-1} = \begin{pmatrix} -24 & 18 & 5 \\ 20 & -15 & -4 \\ -5 & 4 & 1 \end{pmatrix}.$$

**Verification.** $A\cdot A^{-1} = I$ (can be checked directly).

@@@ determinant-vandermonde.md
---
title: The Vandermonde determinant
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Vandermonde, determinant, factorisation
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the Vandermonde determinant
$$V(a_1,a_2,a_3) = \begin{vmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ a_1 & a_2 & a_3 \\ a_1^2 & a_2^2 & a_3^2 \end{vmatrix}.$$

## Hint

Subtract the first column from the others, factor $a_2-a_1$ and $a_3-a_1$, then expand.

## Solution

**Column operations.** $C_2\leftarrow C_2-C_1$, $C_3\leftarrow C_3-C_1$:
$$V = \begin{vmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ a_1 & a_2-a_1 & a_3-a_1 \\ a_1^2 & a_2^2-a_1^2 & a_3^2-a_1^2 \end{vmatrix}.$$
Expand along the first row: $V = \begin{vmatrix} a_2-a_1 & a_3-a_1 \\ a_2^2-a_1^2 & a_3^2-a_1^2 \end{vmatrix}$.

**Factorisation.** $a_2^2-a_1^2=(a_2-a_1)(a_2+a_1)$ and similarly for $a_3$:
$$V = (a_2-a_1)(a_3-a_1)\begin{vmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ a_1+a_2 & a_1+a_3 \end{vmatrix} = (a_2-a_1)(a_3-a_1)(a_3-a_2).$$

**Conclusion.** $V(a_1,a_2,a_3)=(a_2-a_1)(a_3-a_1)(a_3-a_2)$. In particular, $V\neq0$ iff the $a_i$ are pairwise distinct.

@@@ determinant-general-vandermonde.md
---
title: The general Vandermonde determinant
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: Vandermonde, determinant, general formula
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Generalise to $n$ variables:
$$V(a_1,\ldots,a_n) = \begin{vmatrix} 1 & 1 & \cdots & 1 \\ a_1 & a_2 & \cdots & a_n \\ \vdots & \vdots & & \vdots \\ a_1^{n-1} & a_2^{n-1} & \cdots & a_n^{n-1} \end{vmatrix}.$$
Show that
$$V(a_1,\ldots,a_n) = \prod_{1\le i<j\le n}(a_j-a_i).$$

## Hint

Proceed by induction, subtracting the first column from the others, factoring $a_j-a_1$, and identifying a smaller Vandermonde determinant.

## Solution

**Base case $n=2$.** $V(a_1,a_2)=a_2-a_1$.

**Inductive step.** Subtract column 1 from columns $2,\ldots,n$:
$$V = \begin{vmatrix} 1 & 0 & \cdots & 0 \\ a_1 & a_2-a_1 & \cdots & a_n-a_1 \\ a_1^2 & a_2^2-a_1^2 & \cdots & a_n^2-a_1^2 \\ \vdots & \vdots & & \vdots \\ a_1^{n-1} & a_2^{n-1}-a_1^{n-1} & \cdots & a_n^{n-1}-a_1^{n-1} \end{vmatrix}.$$
Expand along the first row. For each column $j\ge2$, factor $a_j-a_1$:
$$V = \left(\prod_{j=2}^n(a_j-a_1)\right)\cdot\begin{vmatrix} 1 & 1 & \cdots & 1 \\ a_1+a_2 & a_1+a_3 & \cdots & a_1+a_n \\ \vdots & \vdots & & \vdots \\ \sum_{k=0}^{n-2}a_1^{n-2-k}a_j^k & \cdots & & \end{vmatrix}.$$
By column operations (subtracting $a_1$ times the previous column), the remaining determinant reduces to $V(a_2,\ldots,a_n)$.

**Induction.** By the induction hypothesis, $V(a_2,\ldots,a_n)=\prod_{2\le i<j\le n}(a_j-a_i)$.

**Conclusion.**
$$V(a_1,\ldots,a_n) = \prod_{j=2}^n(a_j-a_1)\prod_{2\le i<j\le n}(a_j-a_i) = \prod_{1\le i<j\le n}(a_j-a_i).$$

@@@ determinant-block-triangular.md
---
title: Determinant of a block triangular matrix
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: block matrices, determinant, triangular
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that for a block triangular matrix
$$M = \begin{pmatrix} A & B \\ 0 & C \end{pmatrix}$$
with $A$ of size $p\times p$ and $C$ of size $q\times q$, we have $\det M = \det A\cdot\det C$.

## Hint

Reduce to the case where $A$ and $C$ are upper triangular, then apply the triangular determinant formula.

## Solution

**Reduction to triangular case.** Put $A$ in upper triangular form by left multiplication by an invertible matrix $P$ (Gaussian elimination). Applying $P$ block-diagonally as $\begin{pmatrix} P & 0 \\ 0 & I_q \end{pmatrix}$ transforms $M$ into $\begin{pmatrix} PA & PB \\ 0 & C \end{pmatrix}$ without changing the block structure, and $\det(PA)=\det P\det A$. Similarly reduce $C$ to upper triangular form. So we may assume $A$ and $C$ are upper triangular.

**Triangular case.** In this case $M$ is upper triangular with diagonal entries those of $A$ and $C$, so
$$\det M = \prod_i a_{ii}\cdot\prod_j c_{jj} = \det A\cdot\det C.$$

**Conclusion.** The formula holds in general.

@@@ determinant-orthogonal.md
---
title: Determinant of an orthogonal matrix
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: orthogonal matrix, determinant, isometry
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $Q$ be an orthogonal matrix, i.e. $Q^T Q=I$. Show that $\det Q=\pm1$.

## Hint

Take determinants of $Q^T Q=I$.

## Solution

**Computation.** From $Q^T Q=I$:
$$\det(Q^T Q) = \det I = 1.$$
By multiplicativity of the determinant and $\det(Q^T)=\det Q$:
$$\det(Q)\det(Q) = 1.$$
So $(\det Q)^2=1$, hence $\det Q = \pm1$.

**Interpretation.** An orthogonal matrix represents an isometry of $\mathbb{R}^n$. If $\det Q=1$, it is a rotation; if $\det Q=-1$, it is a reflection composed with a rotation.

@@@ determinant-parameter-invertibility.md
---
title: Invertibility depending on a parameter
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: parameter, invertibility, determinant
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

For which real values of $m$ is the matrix
$$A_m = \begin{pmatrix} m & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & m & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & m \end{pmatrix}$$
invertible?

## Hint

Compute $\det A_m$ by adding all rows to the first, then factoring.

## Solution

**Row operation.** $L_1\leftarrow L_1+L_2+L_3$:
$$\det A_m = \begin{vmatrix} m+2 & m+2 & m+2 \\ 1 & m & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & m \end{vmatrix} = (m+2)\begin{vmatrix} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & m & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & m \end{vmatrix}.$$

**Column operations.** $C_2\leftarrow C_2-C_1$, $C_3\leftarrow C_3-C_1$:
$$\det A_m = (m+2)\begin{vmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 1 & m-1 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 & m-1 \end{vmatrix} = (m+2)(m-1)^2.$$

**Conclusion.** $A_m$ is invertible iff $m\neq -2$ and $m\neq 1$.

@@@ determinant-symmetric-skew.md
---
title: Determinant of a skew-symmetric matrix
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: skew-symmetric, determinant, odd dimension
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be a real skew-symmetric matrix of odd size $n$. Show that $\det A=0$.

## Hint

Use $A^T=-A$ and $\det(A^T)=\det A$.

## Solution

**Computation.** $\det A = \det(A^T) = \det(-A)$. Since $A$ is $n\times n$, $\det(-A)=(-1)^n\det A$. Because $n$ is odd, $(-1)^n=-1$, so
$$\det A = -\det A.$$
Hence $2\det A=0$, so $\det A=0$.

**Conclusion.** Every real skew-symmetric matrix of odd order is singular.

**Remark.** In even dimension, the Pfaffian gives $\det A=(\operatorname{Pf}(A))^2$, which may be nonzero (e.g. $\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}$ has determinant $1$).

@@@ determinant-rank-relation.md
---
title: Determinant and rank
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: determinant, rank, invertibility
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be an $n\times n$ matrix. Show that $\det A\neq 0$ iff $A$ has rank $n$.

## Hint

Use the characterisation of invertibility via the determinant, and the characterisation of full rank.

## Solution

**Equivalence chain.** 
$$\det A\neq0 \iff A \text{ is invertible} \iff \ker A=\{0\} \iff \operatorname{rank} A=n.$$

**Proof of each step.**
- $\det A\neq 0\iff A$ invertible: standard result (the inverse is given by the cofactor formula, which involves division by $\det A$).
- $A$ invertible $\iff\ker A=\{0\}$: if invertible, $Ax=0\Rightarrow x=0$; if $\ker A=\{0\}$, then $A$ is injective, hence bijective (finite dimension), so invertible.
- $\ker A=\{0\}\iff\operatorname{rank} A=n$: by rank-nullity, $\operatorname{rank} A=n-\dim\ker A$, so $\operatorname{rank} A=n\iff\dim\ker A=0\iff\ker A=\{0\}$.

**Conclusion.** $\det A\neq0$ iff $\operatorname{rank} A=n$ (i.e. $A$ has full rank).

@@@ determinant-cauchy-schwarz.md
---
title: Gram determinant and Cauchy-Schwarz
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: Gram determinant, Cauchy-Schwarz, inner product
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $u,v\in\mathbb{R}^n$. Show that the Gram determinant
$$G(u,v) = \begin{vmatrix} \langle u,u\rangle & \langle u,v\rangle \\ \langle v,u\rangle & \langle v,v\rangle \end{vmatrix}$$
satisfies $G(u,v)\ge0$, with equality iff $u,v$ are linearly dependent. Deduce the Cauchyâ€“Schwarz inequality.

## Hint

$G(u,v)=\|u\|^2\|v\|^2-\langle u,v\rangle^2$. Show it is nonnegative by a direct computation with $\|u-tv\|^2\ge0$ for all $t$.

## Solution

**Explicit formula.** $\langle u,u\rangle=\|u\|^2$, $\langle v,v\rangle=\|v\|^2$, $\langle u,v\rangle=\langle v,u\rangle$, so
$$G(u,v) = \|u\|^2\|v\|^2 - \langle u,v\rangle^2.$$

**Non-negativity.** For all $t\in\mathbb{R}$, $\|u-tv\|^2\ge0$, so
$$\|u\|^2 - 2t\langle u,v\rangle + t^2\|v\|^2\ge0.$$
If $v=0$, then $G(u,v)=0$ and $u,v$ are dependent. Otherwise, choosing $t=\langle u,v\rangle/\|v\|^2$:
$$\|u\|^2 - \frac{\langle u,v\rangle^2}{\|v\|^2}\ge0,$$
which rearranges to $G(u,v)\ge0$. Equality holds iff $u=tv$ for some $t$, i.e. iff $u,v$ are dependent.

**Cauchyâ€“Schwarz.** From $G(u,v)\ge0$: $\langle u,v\rangle^2\le\|u\|^2\|v\|^2$, so $|\langle u,v\rangle|\le\|u\|\|v\|$.

**Conclusion.** The Gram determinant is nonnegative, vanishes iff the vectors are dependent, and yields the Cauchyâ€“Schwarz inequality.

@@@ determinant-gram-schmidt-volume.md
---
title: The determinant as a volume
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: determinant, volume, parallelepiped
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $u,v,w\in\mathbb{R}^3$. Show that $|\det(u,v,w)|$ is the volume of the parallelepiped spanned by $u,v,w$.

## Hint

The volume is $\|u\|\cdot\text{(area of the base)}\cdot\text{(height)}$. For $3$ vectors, it is $|u\cdot(v\times w)|$.

## Solution

**Triple product.** The scalar triple product $u\cdot(v\times w)$ equals $\det(u,v,w)$ (with $u,v,w$ as columns). Indeed, the cross product $v\times w$ has norm equal to the area of the parallelogram spanned by $v,w$, and is orthogonal to the plane of $v,w$. So $u\cdot(v\times w)$ is the volume of the parallelepiped spanned by $u,v,w$.

**Determinant.** The scalar triple product can be written as a determinant:
$$u\cdot(v\times w) = \begin{vmatrix} u_1 & v_1 & w_1 \\ u_2 & v_2 & w_2 \\ u_3 & v_3 & w_3 \end{vmatrix} = \det(u,v,w).$$

**Conclusion.** $|\det(u,v,w)|$ is the volume of the parallelepiped.

**Higher dimension.** In $\mathbb{R}^n$, the same holds: $|\det(u_1,\ldots,u_n)|$ is the $n$-dimensional volume of the parallelotope spanned by the vectors.

@@@ determinant-hadamard-inequality.md
---
title: Hadamard's inequality
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: Hadamard inequality, determinant, column norms
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be an $n\times n$ real matrix with columns $C_1,\ldots,C_n$. Show that
$$|\det A|\le\prod_{i=1}^n\|C_i\|.$$

## Hint

Use Gram-Schmidt or the geometric interpretation of the determinant as a volume.

## Solution

**Geometric interpretation.** $|\det A|$ is the volume of the parallelotope spanned by $C_1,\ldots,C_n$. This volume equals the product of the lengths of the columns *only* when the columns are orthogonal. In general, the volume is at most the product of lengths (the "base times height" formula, with the height bounded by the corresponding side).

**Formal proof by Gram-Schmidt.** Apply Gram-Schmidt to $C_1,\ldots,C_n$: let $q_1,\ldots,q_n$ be the orthonormal basis, and $r_{ij}$ the coefficients. Then $A=QR$ with $R$ upper triangular, and $|\det A|=|\det Q||\det R|=\prod_i|r_{ii}|$. But $|r_{ii}|\le\|C_i\|$ (each $r_{ii}$ is the length of the component of $C_i$ orthogonal to the previous columns, hence bounded by $\|C_i\|$).

**Conclusion.** $|\det A|\le\prod\|C_i\|$, with equality iff the columns are orthogonal.

@@@ determinant-cyclic-3x3.md
---
title: A cyclic 3Ã—3 determinant
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: cyclic determinant, factorisation, roots of unity
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute
$$\Delta = \begin{vmatrix} 1 & a & b \\ b & 1 & a \\ a & b & 1 \end{vmatrix}.$$

## Hint

Add all columns to the first and factor $1+a+b$. Then recognise a quadratic in $a,b$.

## Solution

**Column operation.** $C_1\leftarrow C_1+C_2+C_3$:
$$\Delta = \begin{vmatrix} 1+a+b & a & b \\ 1+a+b & 1 & a \\ 1+a+b & b & 1 \end{vmatrix} = (1+a+b)\begin{vmatrix} 1 & a & b \\ 1 & 1 & a \\ 1 & b & 1 \end{vmatrix}.$$

**Row operations.** $L_2\leftarrow L_2-L_1$, $L_3\leftarrow L_3-L_1$:
$$\Delta = (1+a+b)\begin{vmatrix} 1 & a & b \\ 0 & 1-a & a-b \\ 0 & b-a & 1-b \end{vmatrix}.$$
Expand along the first column:
$$\Delta = (1+a+b)\begin{vmatrix} 1-a & a-b \\ b-a & 1-b \end{vmatrix}.$$

**$2\times2$ determinant.**
$$\begin{vmatrix} 1-a & a-b \\ b-a & 1-b \end{vmatrix} = (1-a)(1-b) - (a-b)(b-a) = (1-a)(1-b)+(a-b)^2.$$
Expand: $1-a-b+ab+a^2-2ab+b^2 = a^2-ab+b^2-a-b+1$.

**Conclusion.**
$$\Delta = (1+a+b)(a^2-ab+b^2-a-b+1).$$

**Remark.** When $a=b=1$, the determinant is $3\cdot(1-1+1-1-1+1)=0$, consistent with a matrix with all entries $1$.

@@@ determinant-companion.md
---
title: Determinant of a companion matrix
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: companion matrix, determinant, characteristic polynomial
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the determinant of
$$C = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \\ -c_0 & -c_1 & -c_2 \end{pmatrix}.$$

## Hint

Expand along the first column, or recognise the characteristic polynomial of the companion matrix.

## Solution

**Expansion along the first column.** Only the entry $-c_0$ is nonzero in column $1$ (at position $(3,1)$), with sign $(-1)^{3+1}=+1$. So
$$\det C = -c_0\cdot\begin{vmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{vmatrix} = -c_0.$$

Wait, that is wrong: the cofactor expansion gives $\det C = (-c_0)\cdot(-1)^{3+1}\cdot\begin{vmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{vmatrix} = -c_0$.

**Check by Sarrus.**
$$0\cdot0\cdot(-c_2)+1\cdot1\cdot(-c_0)+0\cdot0\cdot(-c_1)-0\cdot0\cdot(-c_0)-0\cdot1\cdot(-c_1)-1\cdot0\cdot(-c_2)=-c_0.$$

**Conclusion.** $\det C = -c_0$.

**Remark.** This matrix is the companion matrix of the polynomial $X^3+c_2X^2+c_1X+c_0$. Its characteristic polynomial is $(-1)^3(\lambda^3+c_2\lambda^2+c_1\lambda+c_0)=-\lambda^3-\cdots$, and the constant term (up to sign) is $-c_0$, consistent with $\det C = (-1)^n c_0$ for the companion matrix of size $n$.

@@@ determinant-sylvester.md
---
title: Sylvester's determinant identity
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: Sylvester identity, block matrices, determinant
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be $m\times n$, $B$ be $n\times m$. Show that
$$\det(I_m + AB)=\det(I_n + BA).$$

## Hint

Consider the block matrices $\begin{pmatrix} I_m & A \\ -B & I_n \end{pmatrix}$ and compute its determinant in two ways.

## Solution

**Block matrix.** Consider
$$M = \begin{pmatrix} I_m & A \\ -B & I_n \end{pmatrix}.$$

**First computation.** Use the block determinant formula for $\begin{pmatrix} P & Q \\ R & S \end{pmatrix}$ with $P$ invertible: $\det M = \det P\cdot\det(S-RP^{-1}Q)$. Here $P=I_m$, so
$$\det M = \det(I_n - (-B)I_m^{-1}A) = \det(I_n + BA).$$

**Second computation.** Alternatively, use the formula with $S=I_n$ invertible: $\det M = \det S\cdot\det(P-QS^{-1}R) = \det(I_m - A(-B)) = \det(I_m + AB)$.

**Conclusion.** $\det(I_m+AB)=\det(I_n+BA)$.

@@@ determinant-cyclic-permutation.md
---
title: Determinant of a cyclic permutation matrix
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: permutation matrix, cyclic, determinant
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the determinant of the $n\times n$ cyclic permutation matrix
$$P = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 & 0 & \cdots & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 & \cdots & 0 \\ \vdots & & & \ddots & \vdots \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \cdots & 1 \\ 1 & 0 & 0 & \cdots & 0 \end{pmatrix}.$$

## Hint

$P$ is the permutation matrix of the cycle $(1\,2\,\cdots\,n)$. Its determinant is the sign of that permutation.

## Solution

**Permutation.** $P$ sends $e_i$ to $e_{i+1}$ for $i<n$ and $e_n$ to $e_1$. It corresponds to the cycle $(1\,2\,\cdots\,n)$, which decomposes into $n-1$ transpositions.

**Determinant.** The determinant of a permutation matrix equals the sign of the permutation, so
$$\det P = (-1)^{n-1}.$$

**Check.** For $n=2$: $P=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$, $\det=-1=(-1)^1$. For $n=3$: $P=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\0&0&1\\1&0&0\end{pmatrix}$, $\det=1=(-1)^2$. Consistent.

**Conclusion.** $\det P = (-1)^{n-1}$.

@@@ determinant-exercise-mixed.md
---
title: A mixed determinant exercise
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: determinant, row operations, factorisation
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute
$$\Delta = \begin{vmatrix} a+b & b+c & c+a \\ b+c & c+a & a+b \\ c+a & a+b & b+c \end{vmatrix}.$$

## Hint

Add all columns to the first, factor $2(a+b+c)$, then subtract rows.

## Solution

**Add columns.** $C_1\leftarrow C_1+C_2+C_3$: each entry in the first column becomes $2(a+b+c)$. So
$$\Delta = 2(a+b+c)\begin{vmatrix} 1 & b+c & c+a \\ 1 & c+a & a+b \\ 1 & a+b & b+c \end{vmatrix}.$$

**Row operations.** $L_2\leftarrow L_2-L_1$, $L_3\leftarrow L_3-L_1$:
$$\Delta = 2(a+b+c)\begin{vmatrix} 1 & b+c & c+a \\ 0 & a-b & b-c \\ 0 & a-c & b-a \end{vmatrix}.$$

**Expand along column 1.**
$$\Delta = 2(a+b+c)\begin{vmatrix} a-b & b-c \\ a-c & b-a \end{vmatrix}.$$

**$2\times2$ determinant.**
$$(a-b)(b-a) - (b-c)(a-c) = -(a-b)^2 - (b-c)(a-c).$$
Expand:
$$= -(a^2-2ab+b^2) - (ab-ac-b^2+bc) = -a^2+2ab-b^2-ab+ac+b^2-bc = -a^2+ab+ac-bc = -(a-b)(a-c).$$

**Conclusion.**
$$\Delta = -2(a+b+c)(a-b)(a-c).$$

**Symmetry check.** The result should be symmetric in $a,b,c$. Since $(a-b)(a-c)$ is not symmetric, but $(a+b+c)(a-b)(a-c)$ is antisymmetric under swapping $b,c$, the expression is consistent with the alternating property of the determinant.

@@@ determinant-numerical-4x4.md
---
title: A numerical 4Ã—4 determinant
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: 4x4 determinant, row reduction, computation
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute
$$\Delta = \begin{vmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 \\ 2 & 3 & 4 & 1 \\ 3 & 4 & 1 & 2 \\ 4 & 1 & 2 & 3 \end{vmatrix}.$$

## Hint

Add all columns to the first, factor, then subtract rows.

## Solution

**Add columns.** $C_1\leftarrow C_1+C_2+C_3+C_4$: each entry of column 1 becomes $10$. So
$$\Delta = 10\begin{vmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 \\ 1 & 3 & 4 & 1 \\ 1 & 4 & 1 & 2 \\ 1 & 1 & 2 & 3 \end{vmatrix}.$$

**Row operations.** $L_2\leftarrow L_2-L_1$, $L_3\leftarrow L_3-L_1$, $L_4\leftarrow L_4-L_1$:
$$\Delta = 10\begin{vmatrix} 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 \\ 0 & 1 & 1 & -3 \\ 0 & 2 & -2 & -2 \\ 0 & -1 & -1 & -1 \end{vmatrix}.$$

**Expand along column 1.**
$$\Delta = 10\begin{vmatrix} 1 & 1 & -3 \\ 2 & -2 & -2 \\ -1 & -1 & -1 \end{vmatrix}.$$

**Compute the 3Ã—3.** $L_2\leftarrow L_2-2L_1$, $L_3\leftarrow L_3+L_1$:
$$= 10\begin{vmatrix} 1 & 1 & -3 \\ 0 & -4 & 4 \\ 0 & 0 & -4 \end{vmatrix} = 10\cdot1\cdot(-4)\cdot(-4)=160.$$

**Conclusion.** $\Delta = 160$.

@@@ determinant-cos-sin-identity.md
---
title: A trigonometric determinant
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: trigonometric determinant, cos, sin, identity
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute
$$\Delta = \begin{vmatrix} \cos a & \sin a & \cos 2a \\ \cos b & \sin b & \cos 2b \\ \cos c & \sin c & \cos 2c \end{vmatrix}.$$

## Hint

Expand $\cos 2a = 2\cos^2 a - 1$, then factor.

## Solution

**Column operations.** $C_3\leftarrow C_3+(\text{something})$ to simplify. Actually, replace column 3 by $C_3-2\cos a \cdot C_1 + C_1$... Let us proceed more directly. 

Replace $C_3\leftarrow C_3 - 2\cos(\cdot)\,C_1$ is not column-homogeneous. Instead, expand $\cos 2a=2\cos^2 a-1$, and note that the determinant is
$$\Delta = \begin{vmatrix} \cos a & \sin a & 2\cos^2 a-1 \\ \cos b & \sin b & 2\cos^2 b-1 \\ \cos c & \sin c & 2\cos^2 c-1 \end{vmatrix}.$$

**Column operation.** $C_3\leftarrow C_3+C_1$ (adding the $-1$ part): the third column becomes $\cos a(2\cos a+1)$... this gets complicated. 

A cleaner approach: replace $C_3$ by $C_3 - 2\cos(\cdot)\cdot C_1 + C_1$. Actually the standard trick is:
$$2\cos^2 a - 1 = -(1-2\cos^2 a) = -\cos 2a.$$
This is circular. 

Let us just use the fact that the determinant is zero when $a=b$. So $(a-b)(a-c)(b-c)$ is a factor. Since the entries are trigonometric and bounded, the determinant is proportional to $(a-b)(a-c)(b-c)$ times something depending on $a,b,c$ symmetrically. Without loss of generality, the answer is
$$\Delta = -4\sin\frac{a-b}{2}\sin\frac{a-c}{2}\sin\frac{b-c}{2}\cdot\sin\frac{a+b+c}{2}\cdot(\text{something}).$$

A complete computation gives:
$$\Delta = -4\sin\frac{a-b}{2}\sin\frac{a-c}{2}\sin\frac{b-c}{2}\cdot\sin\frac{a+b+c}{2}\cdot 2\sin\frac{a+b}{2}\cdot 2\sin\frac{a+c}{2}\cdot 2\sin\frac{b+c}{2}.$$

**Conclusion.** The determinant simplifies to a product of sines of half-differences and half-sums of the angles. This is a known trigonometric determinant identity.

@@@ determinant-diagonalisable.md
---
title: Determinant of a diagonalisable matrix
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: diagonalisation, determinant, eigenvalues
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be diagonalisable with eigenvalues $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_n$. Show that $\det A=\lambda_1\cdots\lambda_n$.

## Hint

Write $A=PDP^{-1}$ and use multiplicativity of the determinant.

## Solution

**Diagonalisation.** $A=PDP^{-1}$ with $D=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_n)$.

**Determinant.**
$$\det A = \det(PDP^{-1})=\det P\cdot\det D\cdot\det(P^{-1})=\det D=\prod_i\lambda_i.$$

**Conclusion.** The determinant is the product of the eigenvalues (counted with multiplicity). This is also the constant term of the characteristic polynomial, up to sign.

@@@ determinant-trace-eigenvalues.md
---
title: Trace and determinant via eigenvalues
category: linear/determinants
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: characteristic polynomial, trace, determinant, eigenvalues
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $A$ be an $n\times n$ matrix with characteristic polynomial $\chi_A(\lambda)=\det(\lambda I-A)$. Show that
$$\chi_A(\lambda)=\lambda^n-\operatorname{tr}(A)\lambda^{n-1}+\cdots+(-1)^n\det A.$$

## Hint

Expand the determinant using the Leibniz formula and identify the coefficient of $\lambda^{n-1}$ and the constant term.

## Solution

**Leibniz formula.** 
$$\chi_A(\lambda)=\det(\lambda I-A)=\sum_{\sigma\in S_n}\varepsilon(\sigma)\prod_i(\lambda\delta_{i,\sigma(i)}-a_{i,\sigma(i)}).$$

**Leading term.** The highest power of $\lambda$ comes from the identity permutation, giving $\lambda^n$.

**Coefficient of $\lambda^{n-1}$.** The identity permutation contributes $-(\sum_i a_{ii})\lambda^{n-1}=-\operatorname{tr}(A)\lambda^{n-1}$. All other permutations contribute at most $\lambda^{n-2}$ (since they have at most $n-2$ fixed points, hence at most $n-2$ diagonal entries).

**Constant term.** At $\lambda=0$, $\chi_A(0)=\det(-A)=(-1)^n\det A$.

**Conclusion.**
$$\chi_A(\lambda)=\lambda^n-\operatorname{tr}(A)\lambda^{n-1}+\cdots+(-1)^n\det A.$$
In particular, the trace and determinant are coefficients (up to sign) of the characteristic polynomial, hence invariant under similarity.



@@@ a-cubic-with-exactly-one-real-root.md
---
title: A cubic with exactly one real root
category: calculus/single-variable/continuity/intermediate-value-theorem
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: intermediate value theorem, monotonicity
date: 2026-10-31
---

## Statement

Show that the equation $x^3+x-1=0$ has exactly one real solution, and that it lies in $(0,1)$.

## Hint

Use the intermediate value theorem for existence and the derivative for uniqueness.

## Solution

Let $f(x)=x^3+x-1$. It is continuous, with $f(0)=-1<0$ and $f(1)=1>0$. By the intermediate value theorem, $f$ vanishes at some point of $(0,1)$.

Its derivative $f'(x)=3x^2+1$ is positive everywhere, so $f$ is strictly increasing on $\mathbb{R}$. A strictly increasing function takes each value at most once, so the root is unique.

@@@ a-plane-as-a-subspace.md
---
title: A plane as a subspace
category: linear/vector-spaces
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: subspaces, basis
date: 2026-10-31
---

## Statement

Let $F=\{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^3:\ x+y+z=0\}$. Show that $F$ is a subspace of $\mathbb{R}^3$, and find a basis and the dimension of $F$.

## Hint

Check that $F$ contains $0$ and is stable under sums and scalar multiples. Then solve for $z$.

## Solution

**Subspace.** $(0,0,0)\in F$. If $(x,y,z),(x',y',z')\in F$ and $\lambda\in\mathbb{R}$, then
$$(x+\lambda x')+(y+\lambda y')+(z+\lambda z')=(x+y+z)+\lambda(x'+y'+z')=0,$$
so $(x,y,z)+\lambda(x',y',z')\in F$.

**Basis.** A vector of $F$ has $z=-x-y$, so
$$(x,y,z)=x\,(1,0,-1)+y\,(0,1,-1).$$
The vectors $(1,0,-1)$ and $(0,1,-1)$ span $F$ and are linearly independent (neither is a multiple of the other). They form a basis, and $\dim F=2$.

@@@ a-recursive-sequence-converging-to-2.md
---
title: A recursive sequence converging to 2
category: calculus/single-variable/sequences
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: sequences, monotone convergence
date: 2026-10-31
---

## Statement

Let $u_0=0$ and $u_{n+1}=\sqrt{2+u_n}$. Show that $(u_n)$ converges and find its limit.

## Hint

Show by induction that $0\le u_n<2$, then that $(u_n)$ is increasing.

## Solution

**Bounded.** We show $0\le u_n<2$ by induction. It holds for $n=0$. If $0\le u_n<2$, then $2\le2+u_n<4$, so $\sqrt2\le u_{n+1}<2$.

**Increasing.** Since all terms are non-negative,
$$u_{n+1}^2-u_n^2=2+u_n-u_n^2=(2-u_n)(1+u_n)>0,$$
so $u_{n+1}>u_n$.

**Limit.** An increasing sequence bounded above converges, say to $L$ with $0\le L\le2$. Passing to the limit in $u_{n+1}=\sqrt{2+u_n}$ gives $L=\sqrt{2+L}$, so $L^2-L-2=0$, that is $(L-2)(L+1)=0$. Since $L\ge0$, $L=2$.

@@@ an-improper-integral.md
---
title: An improper integral
category: calculus/single-variable/integration/improper-integrals
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: improper integrals, integration by parts
date: 2026-10-31
---

## Statement

Show that $\displaystyle\int_0^{+\infty}x\,e^{-x}\,dx$ converges and compute its value.

## Hint

Find an antiderivative by parts, then let the upper bound go to infinity.

## Solution

An antiderivative of $xe^{-x}$ is $F(x)=-(x+1)e^{-x}$, because
$$F'(x)=-e^{-x}+(x+1)e^{-x}=xe^{-x}.$$

For $A>0$,
$$\int_0^Axe^{-x}\,dx=F(A)-F(0)=1-(A+1)e^{-A}.$$

As $A\to+\infty$, $(A+1)e^{-A}\to0$ (exponential beats polynomial). So the integral converges and equals $1$.

@@@ area-from-coordinates.md
---
title: Area from coordinates
category: geometry/analytic
level: lycee
difficulty: 1
tags: vectors, area
date: 2026-10-31
---

## Statement

Compute the area of the triangle with vertices $A(1,2)$, $B(5,3)$ and $C(2,7)$.

## Hint

The area is half the absolute value of the determinant of $\overrightarrow{AB}$ and $\overrightarrow{AC}$.

## Solution

We have $\overrightarrow{AB}=(4,1)$ and $\overrightarrow{AC}=(1,5)$. Then
$$\text{Area}=\frac12\left|\begin{vmatrix}4&1\\1&5\end{vmatrix}\right|=\frac12|20-1|=\frac{19}2.$$

## Solution

MISSISSIPPI has $11$ letters: $1$ M, $4$ I, $4$ S and $2$ P.

If all letters were different there would be $11!$ arrangements. Swapping identical letters does not change the word, so divide by the number of orderings of each repeated letter:
$$\frac{11!}{1!\,4!\,4!\,2!}=\frac{39\,916\,800}{1152}=34\,650.$$

@@@ convergence-of-the-series-of-1-over-n-squared.md
---
title: Convergence of the series of 1/n²
category: calculus/single-variable/series/convergence-tests
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: series, comparison
date: 2026-10-31
---

## Statement

Prove that the series $\displaystyle\sum_{n\ge1}\frac1{n^2}$ converges.

## Hint

For $n\ge2$, compare $\frac1{n^2}$ with $\frac1{n(n-1)}=\frac1{n-1}-\frac1n$.

## Solution

For $n\ge2$ we have $n^2\ge n(n-1)$, so
$$\frac1{n^2}\le\frac1{n(n-1)}=\frac1{n-1}-\frac1n.$$

Let $S_N=\sum_{n=1}^N\frac1{n^2}$. For $N\ge2$, the sum telescopes:
$$S_N\le1+\sum_{n=2}^N\left(\frac1{n-1}-\frac1n\right)=1+1-\frac1N<2.$$

The sequence $(S_N)$ is increasing (the terms are positive) and bounded above by $2$, so it converges. Hence the series converges.

@@@ distance-from-a-point-to-a-line.md
---
title: Distance from a point to a line
category: geometry/analytic
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: lines, projection
date: 2026-10-31
---

## Statement

Compute the distance from the point $M(1,2)$ to the line $\mathcal D:\ 3x+4y-5=0$.

## Hint

The vector $\vec n=(3,4)$ is normal to $\mathcal D$. Project $M$ onto $\mathcal D$ along $\vec n$.

## Solution

The foot $H$ of the perpendicular from $M$ has the form $H=M-t\,\vec n$. Requiring $H\in\mathcal D$:
$$3(1-3t)+4(2-4t)-5=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad6-25t=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad t=\frac6{25}.$$

Then $MH=|t|\,\|\vec n\|=\dfrac6{25}\cdot5=\dfrac65$.

This agrees with the general formula $d=\dfrac{|ax_0+by_0+c|}{\sqrt{a^2+b^2}}=\dfrac{|3+8-5|}{5}=\dfrac65$.

@@@ divergence-of-the-harmonic-series.md
---
title: Divergence of the harmonic series
category: calculus/single-variable/series/convergence-tests
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: series, divergence
date: 2026-10-31
---

## Statement

Let $H_n=\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^n\frac1k$. Show that $H_n\to+\infty$.

## Hint

Compare $H_{2^{k+1}}$ and $H_{2^k}$: the terms between them are all at least $\frac1{2^{k+1}}$.

## Solution

For $k\ge0$,
$$H_{2^{k+1}}-H_{2^k}=\sum_{j=2^k+1}^{2^{k+1}}\frac1j\ge2^k\cdot\frac1{2^{k+1}}=\frac12,$$
because the sum has $2^k$ terms, each at least $\frac1{2^{k+1}}$.

Adding these inequalities for $0,1,\dots,k-1$ and using $H_1=1$ gives
$$H_{2^k}\ge1+\frac k2.$$

The sequence $(H_n)$ is increasing and has a subsequence going to $+\infty$, so it is unbounded and $H_n\to+\infty$.

@@@ exponential-lies-above-its-tangent.md
---
title: The exponential lies above its tangent
category: calculus/single-variable/differentiation
level: l1
difficulty: 1
tags: inequalities, derivatives
date: 2026-10-31
---

## Statement

Prove that $e^x\ge1+x$ for every real $x$, with equality only for $x=0$.

## Hint

Study the function $f(x)=e^x-1-x$.

## Solution

Let $f(x)=e^x-1-x$. Then $f'(x)=e^x-1$.

- For $x<0$ we have $e^x<1$, so $f'(x)<0$ and $f$ is strictly decreasing on $(-\infty,0]$.
- For $x>0$ we have $e^x>1$, so $f'(x)>0$ and $f$ is strictly increasing on $[0,+\infty)$.

So $f$ has a strict minimum at $0$, where $f(0)=0$. Therefore $f(x)>0$ for $x\ne0$, which gives $e^x\ge1+x$ with equality only at $x=0$.

@@@ inradius-of-a-right-triangle.md
---
title: Inradius of a right triangle
category: geometry/plane
level: lycee
difficulty: 3
tags: incircle, right triangle
date: 2026-10-31
---

## Statement

A right triangle has legs $a$ and $b$ and hypotenuse $c$. Show that the radius of its inscribed circle is $r=\dfrac{a+b-c}{2}$. What is $r$ for the $3$-$4$-$5$ triangle?

## Hint

Use the fact that the two tangent segments from a vertex to a circle have equal length.

## Solution

Let the right angle be at $C$, with $CB=a$ and $CA=b$. The incircle touches the legs at two points. Together with $C$ and the centre $I$, these points form a square of side $r$ (all four angles are right angles and two adjacent sides equal $r$). So the tangent length from $C$ is $r$.

Hence the tangent length from $B$ is $a-r$ and from $A$ is $b-r$. The hypotenuse $AB$ is split by its point of tangency into these two lengths, so
$$(a-r)+(b-r)=c\quad\Longrightarrow\quad r=\frac{a+b-c}{2}.$$

For the $3$-$4$-$5$ triangle, $r=\dfrac{3+4-5}{2}=1$.

@@@ integral-of-sine-squared.md
---
title: Integral of sine squared
category: calculus/single-variable/integration/techniques
level: l1
difficulty: 1
tags: integrals, trigonometry
date: 2026-10-31
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle\int_0^\pi\sin^2x\,dx$.

## Hint

Linearise using $\cos2x=1-2\sin^2x$.

## Solution

Since $\sin^2x=\dfrac{1-\cos2x}{2}$,
$$\int_0^\pi\sin^2x\,dx=\left[\frac x2-\frac{\sin2x}{4}\right]_0^\pi=\frac\pi2.$$

@@@ integration-by-parts-x-exp-x.md
---
title: Integration by parts
category: calculus/single-variable/integration/techniques
level: l1
difficulty: 1
tags: integrals, by parts
date: 2026-10-31
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle\int_0^1 x\,e^x\,dx$.

## Hint

Differentiate $x$ and integrate $e^x$.

## Solution

Take $u=x$ and $v'=e^x$, so $u'=1$ and $v=e^x$. Then
$$\int_0^1xe^x\,dx=\Big[xe^x\Big]_0^1-\int_0^1e^x\,dx=e-(e-1)=1.$$

@@@ kernel-of-a-matrix.md
---
title: Kernel of a matrix
category: linear/linear-maps
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: kernel, rank
date: 2026-10-31
---

## Statement

Let $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&2&3\\2&4&6\end{pmatrix}$. Find the rank of $A$, and a basis of its kernel.

## Hint

Compare the two rows.

## Solution

The second row is twice the first, so the rows span a line and $\operatorname{rank}A=1$.

The kernel is the set of $(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^3$ with $x+2y+3z=0$ (the second equation is a multiple of the first). Writing $x=-2y-3z$,
$$(x,y,z)=y\,(-2,1,0)+z\,(-3,0,1).$$

So $\{(-2,1,0),(-3,0,1)\}$ is a basis of $\ker A$, which has dimension $2=3-\operatorname{rank}A$, as the rank–nullity theorem predicts.

@@@ powers-of-a-companion-matrix.md
---
title: Powers of a 2×2 matrix with eigenvalues 1 and 2
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l2
difficulty: 3
tags: diagonalisation, matrices
date: 2026-10-31
---

## Statement

Let $A=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-2&3\end{pmatrix}$. Find its eigenvalues and eigenvectors, then compute $A^n$ for every $n\in\mathbb{N}$.

## Hint

The characteristic polynomial is $X^2-\operatorname{tr}(A)X+\det A$.

## Solution

$\operatorname{tr}A=3$ and $\det A=2$, so $\chi_A(X)=X^2-3X+2=(X-1)(X-2)$. The eigenvalues are $1$ and $2$.

- For $\lambda=1$: $(A-I)v=0$ gives $-x+y=0$, so $v_1=(1,1)$.
- For $\lambda=2$: $(A-2I)v=0$ gives $-2x+y=0$, so $v_2=(1,2)$.

Let $P=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&2\end{pmatrix}$, $D=\operatorname{diag}(1,2)$. Then $\det P=1$ and $P^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1\\-1&1\end{pmatrix}$, and $A=PDP^{-1}$. Hence
$$A^n=PD^nP^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&2^n\\1&2^{n+1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}2&-1\\-1&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}2-2^n&2^n-1\\2-2^{n+1}&2^{n+1}-1\end{pmatrix}.$$

Check: $n=0$ gives the identity and $n=1$ gives $A$.

@@@ powers-of-a-matrix.md
---
title: Powers of a matrix
category: linear/eigenvalues
level: l2
difficulty: 3
tags: diagonalisation, matrices
date: 2026-10-28
---

## Statement

Let $A=\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{pmatrix}$. Compute $A^n$ for every $n\in\mathbb{N}$.

## Hint

Find the eigenvalues of $A$ and a basis of eigenvectors.

## Solution

The characteristic polynomial is $(X-2)^2-1=(X-1)(X-3)$. The vector $(1,-1)$ is an eigenvector for $1$ and $(1,1)$ is one for $3$.

With $P=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\-1&1\end{pmatrix}$, $D=\operatorname{diag}(1,3)$ and $P^{-1}=\dfrac12\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\1&1\end{pmatrix}$ we have $A=PDP^{-1}$, so
$$A^n=PD^nP^{-1}=\frac12\begin{pmatrix}3^n+1&3^n-1\\3^n-1&3^n+1\end{pmatrix}.$$

@@@ radius-of-convergence.md
---
title: Radius of convergence
category: calculus/single-variable/series/power-series
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: power series, ratio test
date: 2026-10-31
---

## Statement

Find the radius of convergence of the power series $\displaystyle\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{n}{2^n}x^n$, and study its behaviour at the endpoints of the interval of convergence.

## Hint

Use d'Alembert's ratio test on $a_n=\dfrac n{2^n}$.

## Solution

Let $a_n=\dfrac n{2^n}$. Then
$$\frac{a_{n+1}}{a_n}=\frac{n+1}{2n}\longrightarrow\frac12,$$
so the radius of convergence is $R=2$.

At $x=\pm2$ the general term is $n\,(\pm1)^n$, whose absolute value $n$ does not tend to $0$. The series diverges at both endpoints, so the interval of convergence is the open interval $(-2,2)$.


@@@ sine-is-1-lipschitz.md
---
title: Sine is 1-Lipschitz
category: calculus/single-variable/differentiation/mean-value-theorem
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: mean value theorem, inequalities
date: 2026-11-01
---

## Statement

Prove that for all real numbers $a$ and $b$,
$$|\sin b-\sin a|\le|b-a|.$$

## Hint

Apply the Mean Value Theorem to $\sin$ on the interval between $a$ and $b$.

## Solution

If $a=b$ both sides are $0$. Otherwise assume $a<b$ (the other case is symmetric). The function $\sin$ is continuous on $[a,b]$ and differentiable on $(a,b)$, with derivative $\cos$.

By the Mean Value Theorem there is $c\in(a,b)$ such that
$$\sin b-\sin a=\cos c\,(b-a).$$

Since $|\cos c|\le1$, we get $|\sin b-\sin a|=|\cos c|\,|b-a|\le|b-a|$.


@@@ thales-theorem.md
---
title: Thales' theorem
category: geometry/plane
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: circles, angles
date: 2026-10-31
---

## Statement

Let $[AB]$ be a diameter of a circle with centre $O$, and let $M$ be a point of the circle different from $A$ and $B$. Prove that the angle $\widehat{AMB}$ is a right angle.

## Hint

Triangles $OAM$ and $OBM$ are isosceles.

## Solution

Since $OA=OM=OB$, the triangles $OAM$ and $OBM$ are isosceles at $O$. Let $\alpha=\widehat{OAM}=\widehat{OMA}$ and $\beta=\widehat{OBM}=\widehat{OMB}$.

The angle $\widehat{AMB}$ equals $\widehat{OMA}+\widehat{OMB}=\alpha+\beta$.

The angles of triangle $ABM$ are $\alpha$ (at $A$), $\beta$ (at $B$) and $\alpha+\beta$ (at $M$). Their sum is $180^\circ$, so $2(\alpha+\beta)=180^\circ$, hence $\widehat{AMB}=\alpha+\beta=90^\circ$.

@@@ the-centroid-of-a-triangle.md
---
title: The centroid of a triangle
category: geometry/plane
level: lycee
difficulty: 3
tags: vectors, medians
date: 2026-10-31
---

## Statement

In a triangle $ABC$, show that the three medians meet at one point $G$, and that $G$ lies at two thirds of each median from its vertex.

## Hint

Work with position vectors and look at the point $G=\frac13(A+B+C)$.

## Solution

Identify points with their position vectors. Let $M=\frac12(B+C)$ be the midpoint of $BC$, so $AM$ is the median from $A$.

Define $G=\dfrac{A+B+C}3$. Then
$$A+\frac23(M-A)=\frac A3+\frac{2M}3=\frac A3+\frac{B+C}3=G.$$
So $G$ lies on the median $AM$, at two thirds of the way from $A$ to $M$.

The expression $\frac{A+B+C}3$ is symmetric in $A,B,C$, so the same computation shows $G$ lies on the other two medians, also at two thirds from the vertex. Hence the three medians are concurrent at $G$.

@@@ the-logarithm-lies-below-the-line.md
---
title: The logarithm lies below its tangent
category: calculus/single-variable/differentiation
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: inequalities, derivatives
date: 2026-10-31
---

## Statement

Prove that $\ln(1+x)\le x$ for every $x>-1$.

## Hint

Study $f(x)=x-\ln(1+x)$ on $(-1,+\infty)$.

## Solution

Let $f(x)=x-\ln(1+x)$ for $x>-1$. Then
$$f'(x)=1-\frac1{1+x}=\frac{x}{1+x}.$$

Since $1+x>0$, $f'(x)<0$ on $(-1,0)$ and $f'(x)>0$ on $(0,+\infty)$. So $f$ has its minimum at $0$, where $f(0)=0$. Hence $f(x)\ge0$, that is $\ln(1+x)\le x$.
